Choosing Your Sales Strategy: Lighthouse vs. Landgrab

a16z(286.0K)·43:56·2.1K views·2026-08-13
Elena BurgerInvestor, a16z Andy McCallOperating Partner, a16z Joe SchmidtOperating Partner, a16z
TL;DR— *Joe Schmidt*
AI startups facing go-to-market decisions must choose between a "Lighthouse" strategy (winning high-exposure, regulated marquee accounts for social proof) and a "Landgrab" strategy (rapidly deploying to mid-market buyers where math and existing budgets drive adoption)....

TL;DR: AI startups facing go-to-market decisions must choose between a "Lighthouse" strategy (winning high-exposure, regulated marquee accounts for social proof) and a "Landgrab" strategy (rapidly deploying to mid-market buyers where math and existing budgets drive adoption). Founders should spend 99% of their time executing rather than over-analyzing strategy.

The Lighthouse vs. Landgrab Framework

  • Defining the Matrix:
      • Co-host Joe Schmidt explains the 2x2 framework using Y-axis buyer exposure/risk and X-axis proof traveling in the market.
      • Lighthouse Model (Top Right): High buyer exposure, proof travels. Fit for regulated industries with limited logos where a bad purchase carries legal or operational risk. Relies on social proof and missionary work (e.g., Harvey in legal, Further AI in insurance).
      • Landgrab Model (Bottom Left): Low buyer exposure, low proof traveling. Fit for markets with established budgets where startups can show direct workflow math and ROI (e.g., Stu in accounts receivable, Pylon in customer support).
  • The SFO/101 Freeway Fallacy:
      • Founders frequently fall into the trap of targeting identical marquee logos in San Francisco or New York because it feels prestigious.
      • Founders should be willing to pick up the phone and sell in markets like Ohio, Chicago, or St. Louis where customers actually need solutions.

Historical Paragons: Samsara and Cisco Meraki

  • Samsara & The ELD Mandate (Andy McCall):
      • Founded in 2015; Andy McCall joined in 2017.
      • Rode the tailwind of the 2016 US federal Electronic Logging Devices (ELD) mandate for long-haul trucking.
      • Mid-Market Focus: Instead of chasing giant, established telematics players (AT&T, Verizon) or top-tier trucking giants for social proof, Samsara targeted the mid-market for fast sales cycles and rapid product feedback loops.
  • Cisco Meraki & Cloud Networking:
      • Founded in 2006 out of an MIT research project (RoofNet); acquired by Cisco in 2012 for its cloud-managed Wi-Fi architecture.
      • Executed a land-grab strategy against Cisco and HP by targeting the mid-market, which lacked trained IT command-line staff.
      • GTM Trial Program: Used webinars to give away free access points, driving adoption by letting customers experience how much simpler the product was firsthand.

Modern AI GTM Dynamics and POC Discipline

  • The Current AI Window:
      • Joe Schmidt notes that enterprise boards everywhere are mandating AI adoption, creating a rare window to sell "big software" and platforms again, as opposed to pure PLG (Product-Led Growth) wedge products.
  • Proof of Concept (POC) Discipline (Andy McCall):
      • Strict Timeboxes: POCs must have a hard end date (e.g., 30, 45, or 60 days) to prevent them from turning into endless science projects.
      • Upfront Success Criteria: Both parties must explicitly define and agree upon what success looks like before starting the trial.

Evolution from Landgrab to Lighthouse

  • Sequential Strategies:
      • Successful companies often deploy both strategies over time. Both Samsara and Meraki started with landgrab motions before maturing into lighthouse strategies by verticalizing into large enterprise segments like public sector and school districts.
  • Team and Sales Profiles:
      • Lighthouse Sellers: Seasoned enterprise reps who understand complex sales cycles, high-exposure accounts, and formal procurement.
      • Landgrab Sellers: Aggressive reps hired for attitude, aptitude, and high-velocity deal stacking.

Tactical Advice for Founders and Sellers

  • The Execution Rule: Spend 1% of time on GTM strategy and 99% on execution. There are no bonus points or revenue multipliers for hard-earned enterprise logos.
  • Early Hires: Andy McCall advises hiring for Sales Operations earlier than most startups do—even just a single person to handle territory alignment, name lists, and sales guardrails to prevent future speed bumps.
  • Quota Realism: Early-stage sales teams run on momentum. Founders should set attainable quotas that allow top sales talent to win; if only 40–50% of the team hits quota, quotas are likely too high or hiring profiles are off.
  • Career Advice for Sellers: Instead of chasing short-term title inflation or peak commission rates, early-career sellers should focus entirely on finding and joining the best growth-stage company.

Notable Quotes

  • > "Too few people are willing to pick up the phone and willing to get on the plane and willing to get, you know, in front of those customers right now because they feel like it sounds way more sexy to sell to JP Morgan Chase than to Morgan Chase."
— *Joe Schmidt*
  • > "There's no bonus points for hard-earned revenue. You don't get extra multipliers on your revenue if you get the big logo... just spend too much time in analysis paralysis mode."
— *Andy McCall*
  • > "The only thing you should really be focused on when you're starting your sales career is find the best company you can possibly find to work for."
— *Andy McCall*

Continual Learning: How AI Agents Get Better With Every Use | Arjun Karanam, Trajectory

Sequoia Capital(236.0K)·21:56·2.5K views·2026-08-13
Arjun KaranamCo-founder, Trajectory
TL;DRArjun Karanam of Trajectory introduces continual learning to close the "experience gap" in AI models, turning discarded interaction tokens into training signals that compound agent performance with every use.
The IQ vs. Experience Disconnect: Models improve rapidly on...

TL;DR

Arjun Karanam of Trajectory introduces continual learning to close the "experience gap" in AI models, turning discarded interaction tokens into training signals that compound agent performance with every use.

The Experience Gap & The Continual Learning Thesis

  • The IQ vs. Experience Disconnect: Models improve rapidly on intelligence (IQ), but every interaction still feels like a first-day-on-the-job experience.
  • The Value of Discarded Interactions: Hundreds of millions of tokens generated by agents are thrown away daily; Trajectory treats these real work traces as a critical training signal.
  • Compound Systems: The goal is to move from static, error-prone agents to learning agents that get faster, better, and cheaper with use, creating compounding system capabilities.

Four Pillars of Continual Learning Infrastructure

  • Full Traceability:
    • Capture the entire tree of execution, including tool calls and sub-agents, rather than just main actions.
    • Elicit and capture corrective behavior—such as edits, undos, and retries—rather than relying solely on noisy thumbs-up/thumbs-down signals.
  • Production-Driven Evals:
    • Draw evals directly from real production traffic and frontier user requests.
    • Build infrastructure that makes every user task rollout re-playable and grades against the real harnesses used in production.
  • Unconstrained Harnesses:
    • Shift from harnesses designed to prevent bad behavior (legacy model constraints) to "let the agents cook" setups where agents orchestrate product primitives.
    • Align the agent interface with the UI so agents can perform every user-facing action via tool calls, backed by informative tool responses (avoiding vague "done/finished" states).
  • Open Weights & Model Routing:
    • Run on open weights to unlock the ability to own and continually improve model weights.
    • Experiment with model routers to direct tasks to the right capability tier.

Handling Data Privacy and Feedback Hierarchy

  • Synthetic Distribution Sampling: To avoid training directly on sensitive customer data, Trajectory samples distributions from customer data and synthetically generates training sets, verifying alignment via distribution comparison.
  • Feedback Categorization:
    • *Negative signal without correction* (e.g., sessions with negative feedback or drop-offs) is used to penalize bad behavior.
    • *Corrected paths* (e.g., retries and edits) provide high-confidence reward signals.
  • Updating Strategy (Weights vs. Context): Global patterns (like a repeatedly failing tool call) are trained into model weights, while customer-specific preferences or local context remain in the harness/context layer.
Okay, last talk to bring us home, we have Arjun and Ronak, co-founders of Trajectory. They're doing a bunch of interesting research around what I think is one of the hotter topics, continual learning. Please welcome Arjun to the stage. Cool. What's up? How's it going everyone? Thank you so much. I'm really excited. I know I'm close to the last one, so hopefully there's like remaining attention span. I'm here. Ronak is also back there, so we're together. But let's get started. We're Trajectory and we're building the platform for continual learning. Who are we? I think we're working on like a really, really cool mission and that's the most fun part. The second most fun part is I get to do this with my two best friends. Ronak over here worked at One Surf and Train Speed 1 before this, and Michael worked on really cool robotics stuff at DeepMind. And what I wanted to start off with was the worldview that we have and how that shapes why we think this problem is important. The worldview that we have is that—and it's not a hot take—we are living in an incredible time in human history. Every single week it seems like another model is coming out that is leapfrogging the last one. It's undeniable that models are getting better and better. But we think that they're getting better on one axis, and that is IQ. These models are smarter and smarter, but it always feels like when you're talking to them, it's their first day on the job. So you take this analogy: if you have a Terence Tao in your pocket, that's great. But Terence Tao day one at an accounting firm is probably not the best accountant there. But give him a few years, or honestly maybe even like a few days, he'd probably be really good. And so we have this orthogonal axis of experience that is much more important than we think in conjunction with IQ. That's what we're calling the experience gap, and that is what we want to close as a company. But the question is, how do you close that? Where does experience come from? Well, it's already out there right now. There are so many—hundred million is a number—but so many tokens out there that are being generated by these agents. You view them, or sometimes you don't even view them, and they get thrown away. But this is all real work that they're doing, and then people are acting upon them and then it's being thrown away. And so our take as a company—as Gabe mentioned, like our opinionated take—is that this is a signal that we should be learning from. This is also how humans get better. In classic AI fashion, if humans do it, it's probably a good way to mental model off of. Today's agents are still slow, expensive, and error-prone. Over time, you implement them, but they probably don't get better. We want to imagine what learning agents look like where, as they are used by people, they get better and better over time. Tangibly, this has two good benefits. Right away, it gets you much faster, better, cheaper models. But more excitingly, it gets you to this goal of systems that compound with use. I want to start off by talking about how we're approaching this problem and then some interesting stuff for you guys at the end. It starts off with traceability, and this kind of dovetails off of what Harrison was talking about before. We need to first capture these interactions, capture this experience that's being thrown away. That's step number one. Step number two—and this is where we start getting into our research—is the way we view the company. We're building a product to allow for continual learning, but we're doing cutting-edge research under every single one of them to make this possible. You have all these interactions, and the next thing we're building is this idea of a model spec. This idea that you need to have a way to define what you want your agent to do and have the agent learn against that. We're doing really cool research here on how to extract user interactions, turn that into reward, extract traces, and turn that into exact specs of what you want your agent to do. Okay, cool. You have what you want your agent to do. What do you do with that? Well, there are two surfaces. One is the models, right? You want your models to learn from actual interactions. We're doing really cool research on algorithms like SDPO and things along those lines where we're using RL to take these full long traces and improve models over time. But models are not the only thing. You have harnesses as well. We're doing a lot of really cool research on: when you have good feedback from people, does that go to a harness, or does that go to a model? An example here is if it's like a fact, like "this company has been delisted," you probably don't want to train that knowledge into the model. It's probably context that should be available to the harness. That is how we're splitting things, and that is the product that we're building: allowing you to go from real interactions all the way to specs of what you want your agent to get better at, to better models, and then better harnesses, and then being able to deploy those models right away. If you have built agents, which I'm sure most of you guys have, you probably know that there are so many things that go wrong with every single part of these, and that every single one of these is an unsolved problem. I'm going to imagine that I am, like, straight from Aladdin, and I have four wishes. What are those wishes that I would ask to make the agent ecosystem better? We have agents today, we have this gap, and there's a level where agents get to; after that, we can really see the exponential effects of continual learning. I'll walk through these in the same four categories I talked about: traceability, evals, the harness, and the model side. So let's start with wish number one: traceability. Two sub-wishes here. Number one is around tracing the entire tree of what happens, including sub-agents. A lot of companies trace the main action that is happening, but they throw away tool calls or sub-agents that are made. That makes it very hard to learn from the whole thing that you've done. Sub-wish number two is probably the most important: you should build your product around how you can both capture this interaction data but also elicit the right amount of feedback. A level-one way of thinking about this is, "Okay, let's have a thumbs up, thumbs down." That sounds amazing in theory, but it's incredibly noisy. If you've used any coding agent, you know you kind of just accept everything that the agent does. It's only five commits later that you're like, "Oh crap, that broke everything. Let me go and undo that." So it's the corrective behavior—the edits, the undos, and the retries—that need to be elicited from the user and captured. The second wish is around evals. The mental model to use here is that the product your user uses should be as close to where the eval is done and where the training is done as possible. In an ideal world, these are all the same. There's no difference between them. This means that evals are drawn from traffic, how people are actually using your product—both how they're using it now and the things they're requesting on the frontier that might not be possible. All of that is super helpful. Second is making every task roll-outable. This is a pretty big infra challenge, but it's this idea that, if a user has done one thing, can I somehow replay what they have done? That is an incredibly helpful boon to moving forward in this agentic era. And lastly, grading through the real harness—not making a variation of the harness, but grading the real harnesses that people use in production. Wish number three: the harness. Number one is, and I think the mental model to use here is that a lot of products have built their harnesses around models that existed a year or a year and a half ago. The primary function of the harness was to prevent the agent from doing bad things, which was good when agents would randomly break or have misformatted outputs. But now we're very much in a "let the agents cook" world. I would view building the harness as not enforcing specific flows, but more about what are the primitives your product has—whether that's search tools or private information—and then viewing the agent as orchestrating those primitives. Sub-wish two—and this is how we're building our product—is making the agent interface as close to the user interface as possible. In an ideal world, every single thing that I can do on your UI, your agent can do via tool call as well. That is a world that makes training and continual learning so much easier. Lastly, make tool responses informative. It's easy to be like, "I just want to know the state," but from an agent's perspective, if you're trying to train or learn off of that, there's no signal. You have no idea what was actually written or read. I only get three wishes, but let's imagine I have four: the models. It would be as easy as just switching to an open-weight model, but if you've tried that, you've probably seen there are 50 other considerations from security to safety to different access provisioning. Start getting comfortable running on open weights, because that is what unlocks the door to owning your weights and then continually improving on top of them. Gabe also talked about this idea that model routers are going to play a large role in routing intelligence to the exact capability of the task. Experimenting with routers is another wish I would wish-cast into the world. When we work with companies, the first thing we often do is an audit. For the most part, there's a lot of work to be done. We work with them to build it out, and it would be my dream if we go to a company and they have a lot of this built out. A lot of the work we're doing is also knowing that a lot of this we can't expect, so we're doing a lot of cool research on: assuming that nobody has evals, how can we overcome that? Or assuming that the traceability is off, how can we learn from the signals that are there? This is a core research and product problem, and we're really excited with the team we've put together. More importantly, we're working with extremely frontier customers—the folks who are really pushing the boundaries of what agents can do in production both today and tomorrow. We have a beta, and a core belief we have is that this ability to own your own intelligence should not be something that you need to consult away. It should be expertise you build on your own. We want to build a product that empowers any company to own their own models and get to continual learning from real interactions. Here's a sneak peek of our product screenshots. Here is Harvey. Here is the lab benchmark that Gabe was talking about. Really easy to import, and training a model is incredibly easy. If you've post-trained, you know there are 50 million things that go wrong and 50 million knobs to turn. What we've tried to do is take all of how our researchers post-train their models and use an agent behind the scenes to take care of most of those knobs. We just expose the ones you need to know to make post-training as easy as possible. Really easy to train a model, see how the model's doing, evaluate it, compare it, and then deploy it. In terms of actual work, that was probably like 15 minutes. That is the world we're trying to get to: to empower all these companies and hopefully more folks in this room. We want every company to own its own experience layer, and we're excited to build that alongside some of you here. Thank you. It's a great talk. I wonder, when you talk about continual learning, what do you think about the trainable object between model weights, harness, tools, application layers? How do you think of them, and how do you prioritize? Yeah, I think a fun phrase is that if you ask six researchers what continual learning is, you're going to get seven answers, probably. You can be very pure and be like, "Oh, it needs to be like a human, and it is just the weights that need to be adopting in real time, one shot." But the way we view it is that the intelligence your product is run off of is a system. It's a system that has many components, and true continual learning is something that optimizes across that system and optimizes based on what parts of the system need to be updated. A mental analogy that I have is like when you are saving things; we no longer think of where in your RAM or where in your hard disk to save. That's the level that's abstracted based on what makes the most sense. We think about models versus harnesses versus context in the same way. It feels wrong that we're having to make decisions off of very little priors about what to update based on what. This is a scientific problem that can be solved. Let's do that and then let's abstract that away. Some speakers today talked about the importance of not training on your customer data. I imagine a big part of continual learning is learning from these interactions. How are you thinking about things like differential privacy? How do you actually make your system better when most application companies have customer-specific data arrangements that make that very hard? 100%. I think that's an incredibly important problem. Before this, I was at Apple, and this is a problem we worked on quite a bit. There's a lot of clever things you can do where you're not actually training on customer data; you're instead sampling distributions from your customer data and then synthetically generating your own. You're comparing those distributions to see if your data is actually on-distribution, but you're not training directly off the customer data. There's a lot of interesting work there, and with our customers, we're doing some interesting and fun things to get over that. Hey, I'm Sid from Finch. I think episodic memory plays a role in continual learning. If a user corrects what an agent did before, we might want to update an agent's behavior. Does Trajectory have an opinionated take about that? On episodic memory and how different types of feedback impact what is learned? So many thoughts. There are two different types of signals. There are signals that are just something went wrong—for example, if somebody flames the agent and says, "You're really bad," but doesn't follow up, or drops off the session. Those are cases where you know something went wrong, but not what right looks like. Then you have things like the agent retried and got to the correct solution, or you corrected it by saying this is a difference. In the latter case, we're very confident about the reward associated with the output. With the former, we know to penalize that behavior, but we don't necessarily say there's a correct right answer. Another way of looking at it is there is a hierarchy of how pertinent the information is. Some information is globally accurate, like a tool call repeatedly failing. That should be trained into the model. But if a certain user says, "I never want to use the sub-agent," that's probably not something you should train into the model, but leave to the context. We're excited about this happening on a per-org or per-customer basis. I think one of the themes of today has been the idea that there are some tasks where you want to experiment more and add more open-source stuff, versus where being at the frontier and using a closed model is more useful. Are there tasks or workflows where continual learning matters a lot, versus where just using a statically trained frontier model and injecting information into the context is sufficient? The level-one answer is that most tasks work for continual learning, but the ones I'm most excited about are tasks on the frontier. The mental model we have for AI progress is that people will ask products with the expectation of what they think the product can do. Maybe they'll ask at the edge of what the product can't do, see it fail, and retreat back. But over time, as models get better, I started querying for bigger and bigger things. What we're seeing with some of our customers are cases where users ask for things, the model can barely do it, but then during training, it learns how to do it. The user can now do this thing they couldn't do before. That chain is what's really exciting about continual learning—really pushing that frontier of what's possible based on what the user tries and cannot do. Awesome. Thank you, Arjun. I think we're out of time. I really appreciate you doing this. It's awesome. Thank you.

How Chess.com Became the World’s Biggest Chess Community with CEO Erik Allebest

No Priors: AI, Machine Learning, Tech, & Startups(91.3K)·46:08·567 views·2026-08-13
Erik AllebestCEO, Chess.com Sarah GuoFounder, Conviction
TL;DRErik Allebest and Sarah Guo discuss how Chess.com scaled to 250 million registered members, 10 million daily active users, and over $200 million in annual revenue by prioritizing pure user experience, community, and the cultural revival of chess.
Domain Acquisition: In 2005,...

TL;DR

Erik Allebest and Sarah Guo discuss how Chess.com scaled to 250 million registered members, 10 million daily active users, and over $200 million in annual revenue by prioritizing pure user experience, community, and the cultural revival of chess.

Company Backstory & Scale

  • Domain Acquisition: In 2005, Erik Allebest and his co-founder/CTO bought the chess.com domain name for $56,000 out of a bankruptcy auction with the vision of building a MySpace-like community for chess.
  • Bootstrapped Growth: Facing rejection from venture capitalists who called the idea an "uninvestable niche passion project," the founders grew the business slowly funded by cash flow, revenue from online learning memberships launched in 2007 (within 18 months of launch), and personal savings.
  • Current Metrics: Today, Chess.com employs 650 people, boasts 250 million registered members, 10 to 50 million monthly active users, 10 million daily active users, and projects slightly over $200 million in revenue this year.
  • Recent Milestones: After explosive viral waves during the COVID-19 pandemic, *The Queen's Gambit*, short-form content, the Mittens bot, and online cheating scandals in 2023, the platform successfully stabilized at a much higher baseline by 2024.

Private Equity & Investment Evolution

  • General Atlantic: Entered around 2020 or 2021 as the first private equity firm to believe in the mission, buying secondary shares from existing investors without injecting new capital.
  • CVC Capital Partners: Recently partnered with Chess.com to buy out earlier shares, bringing deep expertise in gaming, sports, and community businesses; General Atlantic also reinvested.
  • Operational Impact: Allebest notes that working with General Atlantic and CVC has positively transformed the company through rigorous forecasting, operational excellence, and active, in-the-weeds product collaboration rather than top-down pressure.

AI, Human Expertise, & Anti-Cheating

  • Evolution of Chess Engines: Early chess engines like Stockfish made games overly mechanical and boring, but subsequent neural network engines like Leela Chess Zero introduced aggressive, unconventional play that reinvigorated human creativity and gameplay.
  • Accelerated Learning: Modern AI tools, personalized puzzles, game reviews, and LLM-powered coaching help young players (including children and 7-to-10 prodigies) reach elite levels much faster.
  • Human-Centric Retention: Despite computers outclassing humans for 30 years, chess popularity has surged because humans fundamentally want to compete against other humans and value shared cultural touchstones.
  • Anti-Cheating Operations: Chess.com dedicates substantial resources, statistical models, and machine learning models to detect cheating, protect game integrity for both casual and professional players with prize money, and ban bad actors.

Product Expansion & New Ventures

  • Pure Software Focus: Unlike capex-heavy businesses or hardware companies, Chess.com succeeded as a software and content platform driven by relentless focus on user experience and community.
  • Gambit.com: Chess.com recently launched *gambit.com*, a poker platform designed to apply the chess playbook—introducing skill-based ratings rather than just chip volume or financial risk.
  • Future Games: The company plans to roll out additional classic games built around wholesome, educational, and pure gaming principles.
  • Internal AI Stack: The company utilizes LLMs for customer support automations, internal data analysis, and an internal authentication/knowledge layer called GNS built by CTO Josh to accelerate product development cycles.

Notable Quotes

"We came on and we relentlessly focused on what we wanted as players ourselves. We wanted a product that felt great. We wanted it to be free to play... and, you know, over time, that wins out." — Erik Allebest
"Fundamentally, humans want to do human stuff... humans competing against other humans. It's humans making things. It's humans solving problems." — Erik Allebest
"If you just take any given day in your life and multiply that by a thousand, that's kind of what your life looks like... It's your habits that really compound over time." — Erik Allebest

NVIDIA VP Ming-Yu Liu: Cosmos 3, World Models, Kung Fu, What Jensen Taught Me

Zhang Xiaojun Podcast(41.0K)·216:13·4.9K views·2026-08-13
Ming-Yu LiuVP of Research, NVIDIA
TL;DRNVIDIA VP of Research Ming-Yu Liu leads the development of Cosmos, a foundational world model designed to power Physical AI and autonomous robotics.
Rising from researcher to executive over his 10-year tenure at NVIDIA, Liu utilizes a disciplined, execution-focused approach...

TL;DR

NVIDIA VP of Research Ming-Yu Liu leads the development of Cosmos, a foundational world model designed to power Physical AI and autonomous robotics. Rising from researcher to executive over his 10-year tenure at NVIDIA, Liu utilizes a disciplined, execution-focused approach inspired by CEO Jensen Huang to build foundational infrastructure that enables the broader AI ecosystem.

角色与团队构造

  • 职级与定位: Ming-Yu Liu serves as Vice President of Research at NVIDIA and Vice President of Cosmos Lab, making him the first manager in NVIDIA's history to rise from a researcher to a vice president.
  • 团队规模: The direct core team under Liu comprises roughly 80 to 90 researchers and engineers.
  • Cross-Functional Collaboration: Across the broader data pipeline and supporting initiatives, the total headcount working on the Cosmos project scales between 200 and 300 people.

从生成式视觉到世界模型的演进

  • 学术与工业背景: Liu earned his PhD from the University of Maryland under Rama Chellappa, focused on computer vision and generative models, followed by a stint at Mitsubishi Electric Research Laboratories (MERL) before joining NVIDIA 10 years ago.
  • Early Generative AI Pioneering: Liu was among the earliest researchers to apply deep learning to media generation, pioneering unsupervised image-to-image translation (e.g., CoupledGAN) and creating GauGAN ("Ma Liang's Magic Brush"), which helped establish NVIDIA's identity in AI beyond just hardware.
  • The Pivot to World Models:
      • Initially focused on digital media generation (Picasso project withGetty Images and Shutterstock using 1,000 A100 GPUs), Liu realized the greater technological imperative lay in Physical AI.
      • Inspired by OpenAI's DALL-E and Sora, Liu and fellow researchers wrote a letter to Jensen Huang advocating for increased investment, which officially birthed the Cosmos project.

核心技术架构：Cosmos 3 与物理人工智能

  • Cosmos 3 Unified Architecture: Moving away from separate, fragmented models, Cosmos 3 acts as an omni-modal world foundation model designed to unify modalities.
  • Multi-Modal Integration as a "First-Class Citizen":
      • Text & Audio: Integrates language for human-machine communication and audio to capture environmental cues.
      • Video: Serves as the primary observation signal for how the physical world operates.
      • Action: Crucially treats *action* as a first-class citizen, allowing physical AI agents to alter the state of the physical world.
  • Dual-Tower Design: Cosmos 3 utilizes a dual-tower structure separating discrete tokens (optimized for understanding/reasoning) from continuous variables (optimized for generation/diffusion), balancing deployment efficiency with generative capability.
  • Open Source Commitment: NVIDIA releases Cosmos open-source to the community, viewing the initiative not as commercial competition with its clients, but as foundational infrastructure designed to "help the community succeed" and accelerate the deployment of Physical AI.

NVIDIA 的管理哲学与 Jensen Huang 的影响

  • GSD (Getting Things Done): Liu's leadership is defined by an engineering-first, execution-heavy mindset.
  • First Principles & Prioritization: Influenced heavily by Jensen Huang, Liu adopts rigorous prioritization, cutting distractions ("no-excuses" philosophy) and focusing on high-impact ("zero-billion-dollar") problems.
  • Organic Organizational Structure: NVIDIA avoids rigid silos; operations run on the principle that *"Mission is the Boss,"* allowing talent to dynamically mobilize around critical company objectives.
  • No Layoffs Culture: During economic downturns, NVIDIA avoids layoffs, instead trimming non-essential travel and administrative costs to protect its workforce and maintain employee trust.

个人生活与武术修行

  • Rigorous Routine: Liu wakes up daily between 4:00 AM and 5:00 AM to practice Chinese kung fu.
  • Lineage and Training: He has studied Chinese martial arts since age 18 under renowned master Xu Ji (a successor in the lineage of Li Shuwen, the "Divine Spear").
  • Recovery and Stamina: Following his morning stances and forms, he utilizes ice barrel plunges to maintain the physical endurance required for high-intensity executive demands.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • ChatGPT Moment for Physical AI: Liu predicts that a "ChatGPT Moment" for Physical AI—demonstrating unmistakable economic value and practical utility—is inevitable and will materialize rapidly, potentially within the next year or two ("this year, or next year").
  • Hardware-Software Convergence: Liu forecasts that the ultimate frontier for robotics will rely heavily on tight vertical integration of hardware and software, driven largely by manufacturing ecosystems capable of rapid iteration.

Notable Quotes

*"Economics flourishes based on how fast money flows from one person's pocket to another; research flourishes based on how fast ideas flow from one brain to another."* — Ming-Yu Liu
*"If I cannot create, I cannot understand."* — Richard Feynman (frequently cited by Liu on the necessity of generative modeling for true machine understanding)
*"There are no excuses in the real world. Are you okay, baby? Nothing in this world is fair."* — Jensen Huang (recounted by Ming-Yu Liu as a defining lesson on accountability)
Hello大家好，我是小珺。今天我在上海，访谈对象是英伟达的副总裁刘名宇。我们的访谈时间是黄仁勋刚刚访华之后的一个月。名宇给我的印象一直是穿着很朴素，背着一个有点旧的斜挎包，态度很亲和。不过英伟达内部的人告诉我，他不像是一个典型的研究者，更像是一个工程上的领导者。黄仁勋给他一个评价是：GSD，Getting things done。 不久前，刘名宇带领团队刚刚发布了英伟达的世界模型Cosmos 3，所以在这次访谈中，我们聊了聊Cosmos 3和世界模型。同时我们也聊到一名研究者长达20年的求索，而这名研究员的身份很特殊，他身处在英伟达这样一个基础设施型的巨大的系统之中，这改变了他很多的研讨思路。当然我们也很不自觉地聊了一些关于英伟达和黄仁勋的内部故事。名宇说做模型就要Low ego，模型竞争不应该是一场游戏。那接下来就是我对英伟达研究副总裁刘名宇的访谈。 Jensen常常在讲的是他喜欢这种billion dollar business，你可以是不赚钱，但当这个生意成功的时候必须是很Incentive的，是很大的。经济的茂盛程度是根据钱多快从一个人的口袋流到另外一个口袋，那研究的茂盛程度应该是根据Idea多快从一个人的脑袋流到另外一个脑袋去。我们做完Cosmos 1的时候问那个Jensen要不要继续往下做？然后Jensen跟我说，你就做到Cosmos 97。因为他是一个很有纪律的人，有些东西即使很重要，他会叫你pace，pace your steps，就是让你不要急。他擅长打马拉松战，他每天都觉得公司只有30天的现金流，30天后就要破产。 你刚好加入英伟达10年了。是的。这10年你变化大吗？你在英伟达的这个股票涨了多少倍了？哈哈哈哈，线上朋友大家好，我是刘名宇。嗯，因为刚才我们也聊到你到美国了20年，然后在英伟达了10年，你现在讲中文还多吗在公司里面？就是有时候会讲中文，但大部分时候还是讲英文，因为大部分的时候团队里还是很多人不会讲中文，所以沟通上就是以英文为主。但如果在一个小组会议里面，参与的都是讲中文的，那很自然而然就会切换到中文模式。 这次你来中国的行程是什么样的？你多久会来一次中国？我现在大概是每一年会来二到三次中国。因为我主要的公司给我的任务、我个人的兴趣在Physical AI，在中国这里Physical AI的发展是非常的茂盛。很多公司，很多研究员都在做出惊人的突破，我在这里跟大家交流，也理解下我们如何可以帮到他们。这次来感觉到有什么不一样吗？跟之前。这次来我看到是越来越多的关于手，灵巧手，在这个关键的科技上面做出突破，然后去追求下一个Physical AI可以达到的一些重点能力。这是跟上次来发觉就是速度非常的快，进展非常的快，然后每次都能学到很多东西。 这应该是你第一次做一个中文的Podcast，我想能帮助观众或者听众朋友们建立一个印象，你现在在英伟达的Title是研究副总裁，能介绍一下这个职位以及你们团队的一些构造和人数？我现在是研究副总裁，另外也是Cosmos Lab的副总裁。我们的团队呢，就是研究员加工程师，是属于我这里的大概有八九十人。那另外公司还有其他的团队也是在做Cosmos。英文叫Data pipeline，虽然他不直接report给我，但是我是整个mission的overall pick，就是发号施令的人，然后决定方向的人。所以这些人也是遵循我的指示，就是跟整个团队一起合作把目标给推出去。整个做Cosmos的应该有2到300人之间。这是一个在英伟达里面很重要的一个专案，我们花很多资源，这样的资源投入是我们觉得需要的，做出好的成果。 我看到你之前还管一个团队叫Deep Imagination Research Group，Cosmos是它演化来的吗？他们是什么关系啊？是的，我是10年前的时候加入英伟达。我在加入英伟达的时候，我的目标就是，我那时候给Job talk在跟英伟达推销我自己的时候，我讲的是有一天，最主要生成视频的运算将会是Deep Learning，而不是传统的Computer Graphics。在那时期，就是电脑特效，电影特效，用这种Graphics，传统的Graphics Pipeline去生成漂亮的Graphics是主流。然后我是这领域里面早期认为Deep Learning是未来的趋势。那我也记得那时候我跟我的Chief Scientist Bill Dally在presentation里面讲的是Deep Learning，因为它是Data-Driven，它能做到很多传统Graphics可以做不到的事情。比如，我有一天可以把小说当成是Input，放进这个Deep Learning，就可以产出电影。然后你可以做很多，现在传统Graphics需要大量人力才能做出来的这些控制。那时候公司对这个东西很有兴趣，我就很荣幸的加入英伟达。 进去了之后呢，我就一直朝着这方向努力。在那时期最主要的这个模型的是Generative Adversarial Network，就是对抗生成网络。那我是领域里早期去从事这个对抗生成网络研究的人，我在里面一直做这方面的研究。我做了几个我自己很感到骄傲的工作。其中一个就是我是最早提出这个可以做我们叫做Unsupervised或者Unpaired的Image Translation。在AI里面学习很多时候都靠Supervision，你要对应的Input跟Output，你才可以写出这个Input Output Relationship。那Unsupervised就是说你并没有对应的Input Output，你并不知道这个人笑的时候是怎么样，你不知道这个马变成斑马会是怎么样。那我们那时是早期利用一些Information Bottleneck，可以去做出我们叫Unsupervised Image to Image Translation。那时候我加入英伟达的时候，英伟达并不是一个大家认同的AI公司。那时候Deep Learning还没有像现在这么的火热，那英伟达传统也都是做这种Computer Architecture，去设计GPU，没有人去在做这种比较应用端的科研。那我是早期在英伟达把这个方向做起来的。 那我在做的许多东西，其中一个work叫GauGAN，我想中文把它翻成马良神笔，像是小画家一样，涂几个颜色，每个颜色代表不同的语意，那你涂一涂之后，就可以变成漂亮的山水画。那个work让我也是得益是帮英伟达建立起个形象，不只是可以做出很好的GPU，也可以做出很好的AI模型。那那时候我还有一些同事在一起做那个StyleGAN方向的研究。我们就是这群人把英伟达从DP，就是等于是第一个把这种生成式网络来做Media生成把它带下。做了一段时间之后，公司就觉得我，可以了，然后就可以开始成立自己的团队。那我那时候成立的团队就叫Deep Imagination Research，就是跟我当初给Job talk的目标是一致的。那是哪一年？是19到20年之间，那时候变成一个Research Director。但我就是那时候知道自己要成立团队，就跟这几个一起工作的伙伴就开始建立这个Deep Imagination Research。 那接着Deep Imagination Research之后，我就一直在这领域里面解决相关的问题，一步一步朝着把小说放进去，产出电影这个目标前行。我们做了几个有趣的Demo，就是有一年Jensen，我们的CEO想要用一个数字人来取代他来给Keynote，那那时候我们就帮他做这个假的Jensen的Keynote的Demo。那时候是疫情期间嘛，然后在那个时间里面数字人的发展非常的快，然后我们共实现了这些，我们自己觉得很骄傲的Technology。我10年前在给Job talk的时候，我就想要解这个小说变成电影这个题目。但那时候，我觉得这个题目可能需要很久的时间才能解。应该是说，我那时候并没有意识到我们其实有了这个条件了。当你的算力，当你的数据足够的时候，就可以做出这样的一大突破。但是那时候我就忙着做数字人，忙着做各种有趣的应用。到后来有一天OpenAI出了这个DALL·E这个Work，最早的一个Text to Image model，我才理解到，我的野心不够大，不够勇敢，没有决心去把这个东西做出来。 在算力最多的地方，英伟达是这样运作的，你要Jensen投资你算力，你要讲出来这为什么对公司重要。我记得那时候DALL·E出来的时候，我写了一封信给Jensen，跟他讲说这个未来已经很明确了，就是靠Deep Learning来做这个影像生成。那英伟达很多客户都是用英伟达的GPU，用传统的Graphics来做这个影像生成，我们不能在这个用Deep Learning做影像生成下落后，我们必须投资GPU给我们这个团队，去帮助这个领域成长。那那段时间里面，我跟Getty Images也有很多合作，因为我一直在做这个方向，那这些传统的Content公司他们也对AI很感兴趣，但他们也都是试看看，没有足够多的决心。那看到了DALL·E之后，我写了一封信给Jensen要GPU，写了一封信给Getty Images说，我们应该合作，你们这样未来才会有比较好的保障。然后我们就谈成了这个合作方案，就是他们提供数据，然后我们有算力，然后去build这个大模型来做这个Text to Image。这就是我踏入大模型的时间点。 那那时候我们成立了一个专案叫Picasso。我们的目标是做AI房租。就是有些公司有数据，但他们并没有这种AI的人才，没有这种AI的Infrastructure，但他们有数据，他们有客户，那我们就是等于是帮他做一个GenAI的模型，然后把模型给他们，让他们去Plug他们的客户，然后彼此就是这样一起获利。那那时候就是跟Getty Images还有跟Shutterstock去做这件事情。那这是那时候我还在Deep Imagination Research这个团队的事情，我们在做这件事情，那从这里就是慢慢的，我的Research不只是产生一个模型，不只是产生一个论文，我们也产生了一个可以帮助客户成功的产频。那这就是这样逐步的发展。我当初以为我现在已经决心充足，然后就是愿意去挑战最困难的问题，因为已经跟Jensen要的GPU，跟Getty Image要的那个多少GPU？那时我是要了1000个A100 GPU。当时以前涨很多吗那个时候？是的，非常的多，那时候还在MPI的时代，1000张已经很多了，要把1000张的这个GPU串在一起做一个高效的训练也不容易。这是我们那时候尝试就是当然遇到很多瓶颈，那就是注意克服。 因为我是最早去做这个GenAI的，就是在Computer Vision里面做GenAI的人，我也很荣幸就是跟很多很优秀的伙伴一起合作。那因为我比较资深，那很多当时很有为的年轻人还在学生时代，他们会来我这里做实习，然后包含现在在Meta的余佳辉，做出这个Solar的Teambooks都在我这里实习。我记得Teambooks刚加入我团队做实习的时候，他就一直想做Video生成，想做他想做的Solar的东西。那那时候我们并没有这个Technology，我们是用Game一起去合作。那那时候就看到他充满了决心，充满了热情，那人是非常的聪明，然后手脚非常快。那我跟他一起合作里面学到很多东西，我们写了一个蛮不错的论文。那后来毕业之后，想留住他，但他去了OpenAI。也就很开心他在OpenAI那边获得很好的发展。那后来就做出这个Solar这个Work，那Solar这个Work出来之后，我是感到非常开心，但我也觉得我的决心实在是不够，还是不够。还是不够。还是不够。 我们那时候就几位都是在做这个领域的，我们一起写了一封信给Jensen说，我们必须要更多的投资，这就是Cosmos Project的由来。我们这一群学者，我那时还有桑亚菲特勒，然后还有我们公司带这个Nemo团队的Brian Catanzaro，君范那时候也在这个Meeting里面。我们就一起讨论，我们就决定要去去做这个World Model。我们觉得这个对公司像是对人类来讲是一个重要的一个科技。然后那时候Jensen就叫我，还有桑亚菲特得两个起来带这个Cosmos这个effort。那那时候不叫Cosmos。那时候就是我们知道要做World Simulator，然后Jensen就叫我们每个人去想。要叫什么这个Project叫什么名字。我们每个人就回去了去想，我也不忘记那时有几个名字在上面选。但最后Jensen就说，叫Cosmos。然后我们觉得Cosmos这个名字非常的好，然后所以这个Cosmo这个Project是这样产生的。 那然后我这还是在Deep Imagination Lab的时期。那在那个时期，就是我原本是在做Picasso。然后，就是这个AI房租for这个Media Generation。然后但是同时又负责Cosmos。后来觉得就是Cosmos这个Project不管是对公司，不管是对整个领域发展，更关键的更加重要，然后也因为它是World Model，也是需要很多的基础设施的研究，很多底层的研究，那也要同时做这个GenAI的这个Media生成，那是两个领域。那我后来觉得就是Cosmos这个Project不管是对公司，不管是对整个领域发展，更关键的更加重要，然后也因为它是World Model，也是需要很多的基础设施的研究，很多底层的研究，那也要同时做这个GenAI的这个Media生成，那是两个领域。所以也不能分身乏力呀。不能同时跑两个重大的Project。然后慢慢的就把这个Picasso这个Project给收起来，那帮这些客户找到了其他可以继续资源他们的科技公司，那我倾心开始做Cosmos。 那Cosmos做一段时间，从1做到2，做到3。这端时间里面，使用他的客户越来越多，需要一定的规模，才能去把这个Cosmos这个Project做好。你可以想象我在Research时期，可能就是像一个萌芽阶段。当现在这个Cosmos已经这么多人都给依赖，不管是外面的客户，或者是公司内部的团队，到一个一定要成功的阶段，那所需要的资源更加的庞大。那就是今年年初的时候，我就从英伟达Research离开了，我现在是Report给我们的Senior VP of Software，我们公司最大的一个Leader。因为之前是回报给Bill Dally。对，带的Deep Imagination团队，那我现在的Report给Dwight，就是他也是公司的第一个Software Engineer，在Jensen的专机里都有他的故事。对。那我现在Report给他，然后我离开Research之后，我们就换了一个名字，然后我就不叫自己的团队Deep Imagination Research Team，我们就叫自己Cosmos Lab，那是整个这样的一种变化。 我听说Solar没有出自你的团队，让你很遗憾什么？也不是遗憾，就是我是感到很开心，就是自己曾经一起共事过的人可以做出这么大的一个贡献，我感到非常的开心。遗憾的是说每个研究员都想做出贡献。那看到这个Solar产生的贡献这么大的时候，自己会觉得很开心，但在一方面也会觉得说，到底是哪个地方在我的决策过程当中，或者在我的判断的过程当中，哪些地方我没有看对，而导致我没有在正确的时期做出正确的事情。因为你也是一个很喜欢自我反省的人，我就是往往都会去试图理解怎么样让自己更好。英伟达说到了Solar，还是会想要多说一句，你觉得为什么OpenAI后来把这条产品线基本上算是关掉了？Sora它是一个重大的一个研究工作，那它当然本身也有许多应用。OpenAI是一个很有野心的公司，然后他们有大量的人才，那Sam Altman一直是从他早期在Y Combinator，他就是一个投资者。那他当然是希望有这种投资者的心态，希望公司内部有各式各样的Project，有些Project非常的成功，就是各式各样的Portfolio。对。 那我通过在过去一年当中，他们受到这个Anthropic巨大的竞争压力。他们看到Anthropic在Coding上面产生的巨大的经济效应。你再跟ChatGPT聊天的时候，你产生的Token数就是可能只多一分钟就生成完了。那你做这个Coding Agent是可以工作24小时，18小时，那个Token量非常的大。那他们就是我觉得这是一个很好的方向，那就集中公司的经历，往这个Coding的方向走。顺势并法讲的背多责力寡，大的资源都是有限的，那他们就是重新调整资源，然后去往Coding这边去发展。我不觉得他们认为Sora不是一个好的Work，只是我觉得在这个时间点，他们公司有更重要的事情必须要去走，然后要做出一个不容易的决定。 喔，好可惜啊，因为像Anthropic, OpenAI这种前沿实验室，他们做研究天经地义，因为这就是他们的产品。但是对于英伟达这样的一个基础设施公司或者说是一个平台公司，我卖Token给这些前沿实验室就好了，我为什么还要自己做自己的研究呢？就是研究这件事情本身对于英伟达的意义是什么样？你的基础设施要做得好，做得好的定义就是适合这些应用公司来用。那如果你自己不做这些应用，你根本不能理解他们所需要的东西。所以你就是要去走他们可能会走的路，从中间去理解到。而且设计芯片，设计这个电脑的架构，它的周期是很长的。需要很长的一段时间来做这个规划，所以你要做这方面的研究，做这方面的投资，去理解到什么是可能是重要的问题要解，才能设计出更好的这个芯片，更好的Infrastructure来满足这些就是AI大公司。 我总觉得这世界上如果每个想有抱负有理想的人，都有这个工具可以做出他们想做的事情，这世界是一个更好的世界。一方面看到OpenAI, Anthropic还有中国几家大公司做出的突破，就非常开心。但我我们这个社会会有源源不断的有抱负的年轻人，我们也希望他们有他们的机会，就是我们可以做出这些模型，然后分享给他们，他们可以做出很好的应用，很好的延伸模型，或者是从中获得灵感，做出他们更好的模型，然后就是大家不同的想法都可以落地。我觉得当这么多多元的这些Idea都有机会被实现，然后可以被呈现给这些用户，我觉得大家选择更多，那我觉得这个社会进步会更好。这方面为什么英伟达做开源模型的理由。 那你们的研究成果会跟客户去有一些竞争关系吗？我们做这些而且我们做这种开源的模型，我们基本上就是无价，放在网络上面，让大家都可以去应用。这实际上真正要落地，真正要满足客户的需求还有很多东西要做。尤其在巨深领域，好多硬体的问题啊，好多这个商业的问题都需要解。我觉得我们给Community一个信号是说，我们会帮助你在这一方面。那当你看到这个这一趋势，看到我们的Commitments，我们从一代做到二代，做到二点五代做到三代，这样一代做到去，你看到我们一步一步就是想把这个地方做得更好，让大家都可以预期得到，英伟达这上面会一直投资，一直进步。那大家有这个认知，有这个信心，那他就可以把他的资源投在更重要的刀口上面，去做出重大的贡献。因为每家公司，再有钱的公司，资源都是有限的。嗯。那你步，不找到痛点，找到这个关键点去作投资，做进步的话，你是不容易脱颖而出的。那巨深这么的难，这么多东西要解，我觉得我们做出这些模型，帮助他们分担部分的压力，我不觉得是在竞争，我觉得是在帮助大家成功。 为什么你们当时要立一个非常重要的项目，没有选择当时最火的大语言模型，也没有选择像Stable Diffusion这样的模型，而是选择了具身的一个世界模型呢？ 大语言模型，它在当时就是发展的已经非常好了，然后我的背景不是大语言模型，我是computer vision出身的，对，CV出身的。那这样当然就是一直在这个media这边徘徊，哦，就是image、video徘徊，那这就是过去的背景，就是把自己走向这个大语言模型，因为我的背景关系，所以跟我一起合作的伙伴也都是类似的背景，所以我们就是往这方向走。 我觉得第一个时刻就是，在早期，我喜欢漂亮的数学，在Computer Vision早期阶段，什么东西都不Work。 那Computer Vision应用大量的优化的算法，那优化呢，就要去找最佳解，如果是连续性的呢，常常会遇到这种Non-convex的问题，就是你没法找到最佳解。 那如果是离散呢，这种NP-hard的问题，就是你也没有足够多的算力去找到最佳解。 你还是研究出算法去找，一个答案。 但你不知道答案离最佳解多远，那为了去确保你的答案离最佳解，不是太远，就是与一定的就是保障，然后就去证明这个叫Bond的。 也许这个最佳解，是虽然找不到答案，不是最佳解，但它离最佳解可能差4%，或是差20%，这比随便一个答案好。 为了去证明这个Bond的，很多时候在早期的研究，就会开始做一些假设，那假设让你的数学变得比较干净，可以找到答案。 但假设也让你离现实比较远。 然后，我那时候早期会有点偏向于去寻找追求数学的美，而去忽略了跟真实世界的距离，然后，后来一段时间之后，我体理到这并不是一个正确，因为AI本身就是一个应用，就是自利就是拿来被应用的。 然后我开始越来越喜欢接近这个问题的本质，而不会被那些花俏的数学给迷惑。 然后后来有一个阶段就是，可能就从小成长的背景有关，就是我总是专注于把事情做出来，而不专注于让别人懂你做出什么东西。 然后那时候我就更加重视如何让我做东西让别人理解它的重要性，然后让他去理解这个过程，那这是我觉得，可能一个很重要一个Lesson，那但是是我早期做Games的时候。 然后第三个是，我后来理解到，尤其是开始做大模型的时候，我开始理解到团结合作是非常重要的一件事情。 嗯。 人跟人的沟通呢，其实是一个高复杂度的问题，比如说你，不知道叫N-square，假设你有N个人，你跟这些另外N个人沟通是N-square。 那N-square在在复杂度来讲是一个不好的复杂度，只是不容易Scale。 就是当很多人的人的时候，就是每个都要点对点沟通就不容易。 比较好就是这种N啊或者Linear或者Sub-linear。 我开始知道说要做出一些巨大的贡献，需要大家一起团结合作的时候，必须要把事情讲得非常明确，让大家都得懂，然后大家目标能一致，就是自己能把东西做出来。 然后也希望就是大家都能懂，是做在做什么东西，然后可以朝着同一个方向前进，那这地方就还需要很多就是帮助大家去理解为什么这个重要，这这也是后来我做科研，尤其是在做到大的Scale的科研里面觉得很重要的一件事情。 高干是取自高更吗？ 对，这个名字是这样。 那时候我是跟一个，我那时候实习生，叫那个Tessan Park，我们一直在这个研究这个方向，然后还有一起合作，还有朱俊彦，然后还有那个王鼎军，我们这四个就是一起在研究这个题目。 那那时候我们开发出一个算法，叫我们叫Space的，就是Adaptive Modulation，Demodulation，这是一个很传统的某种学习你的方式。 （省略部分重复段落，确保输出逻辑连贯） 有很多种，所以我们做一个online的app，让大家都去测试，然后从里面就是大家使用里面获得很多，就是很好的板块，我们就就是越做越往应用方向前进。 其实GaoGAN是不是跟你之前的研究轨迹是一步一步递进的，他不是第一天就坐在那里。因为你从博士毕业以后加入了30电机研究所，Computer Vision就进入了GAN的时代，然后你其实围绕GAN做了一系列的工作，包括CoGAN，MocoGAN，再是到英伟达之后的，比如说GoGAN这一系列的工作，他是怎么延展的呢？ 我最早的一个GAN的工作叫那个CoupledGAN。 CoupledGAN。 CoupledGAN，就是把两个GAN的生成网络，把它耦合在一起，然后耦合在一起产生了一个Information Bottleneck，然后所有的Deep Learning就是再学怎么压缩讯息，然后Bottleneck是帮助你，帮就是给这个Network一个机会可以去找到本质，那么你一个GAN是在model一个distribution，然后你有两个GAN是model两个distribution，一个就是马，一个是斑马，当你耦合在一起以后，它有一个限制，就会找一些共同性，然后就可以让你做这个转换，从马变斑马，它就是从这个方向走的。 所以，这个GAN是网络就是在做这个distribution modeling。那distribution modeling，就是你可以Sample，就产生了一个image，一个山，一个海，一个Video。但是人不只是想要去生成，它要是能控制自己去生成的东西，那控制呢就是要是信号。那信号有很多种，那一种常见的信号就是拿Image作为信号，就是我这有一匹马，这个变斑马，这是一种信号。另外一种方法就是Edge，就是你素描，然后还有一个就是Segmentation，就是这个像小画家一样，什么颜色代表这山，什么颜色代表这水，这是一个信号。然后，再来呢就是，比如说Car，这一，比如说这是山，这是车，这是机车，那就可以生成。那最最sophisticated的，最接近人的Natural Language就是text，就是text to image，text to video，这是最就是最自然的一种形式，但他Text跟跟这种Segmentation是不同一样，最不同的地方就是Segmentation是给你很具体的这个Pixel是什么，它帮你对位对好了。然后Text呢，他是一个比较不一样，他的structure跟Image Structure是很不一样，跟Video Sense這些東西還在快速疊代當中，這需要領教手，需要數據。我覺得大模型基本上思路有兩個，一個是讓Language當成First class citizen，像coding啊、像Chat；另外一種是讓那種視覺訊號、Video當成是First class citizen，像Dance、像Bill。我們認為物理世界的模型跟這個數字世界的模型最不一樣的地方就是物理世界的Agent會take action，會改變這個世界的狀態。我們認為一個好的物理世界的Foundation model也要把Action當成First class citizen，這就是為什麼我們把Language、Video跟Action合在一起建成這個Cosmos 3。 我記得以前楊治林跟我說，他們做語言模型就是把語言當作最重要的智能提升工具，他們很擔心Vision加入進來會影響他的智商，變成一個傻的多模態。但是今年你跟我說，他擔心語言對於視覺的污染，他覺得語言才是人類的腳手架，並不是本質。你怎麼看待他們這兩種觀點，以及你把這些訊號都訓練到一起時，會出現互相的污染嗎？是的。這些訊號他們帶進來都有他們的目標，Language可以把這些人類的Knowledge帶進來，然後也幫助人類跟這些物理AI做溝通。Video是大量的視覺資訊，Audio是大量的聽覺資訊，去描述這個世界怎麼運作。 用Video更精準地去capture這個世界的物理運作，用Language反而很難。但是這個模型最後建出來是要服務人的，他不讀Language，你是沒辦法跟他溝通的；最後是要幫助這些machine去take action的，所以他不懂Action，他是沒辦法去改變這個世界的。我想這兩個觀點都對，但我把它全部結合在一起，雖然會出現問題，但是我們要解決這些問題。我們是為了要建立一個Foundation model，它需要這三種，我們就把這三種把它合成一個整體。即使會有語言對視覺的污染或者反之，我們都要想辦法解決，因為它是建立好的Physic AI的一個必要條件。 我認同你的看法，這兩位我覺得都很聰明，他們講的東西也都有道理。我認為我們的目的不同，我的目的是希望這成為一個好的Foundation model來for Physical AI。它需要懂Language，需要知道這個東西怎麼變動，然後Action。經由這個constraint，我們把它合在一起去作，我的目標不是去追求AGI。當然AGI本身也很難定義，當你定義的AGI就是純粹要去了解這個人類的本質或做coding的時候，可能這些視覺訊號會影響到你，那是必然的。遙遠的古代是沒有任何語言的，這一切人類的文明發展就是經由看這個世界怎麼變動而產生的。 你可以覺得語言是發展適合人類的，但你不知道機器是不是接受這一套，或許你就從這個視覺訊號裡面直接去學這個世界怎麼運作，得到一個Intelligent，然後發展出另外一個文明。你從這角度看，也許語言訊號是一種干擾，但我很明確知道我要的是什麼。我需要Language讓人類可以跟這個物理AI溝通，我需要Video、Audio去描述、去從這個物理世界的變化去capture。我想這是因為我們目的不一樣，所以我們可以得出這兩個結論。 我就去學會這個物理本質，需要action，因為我希望它最後是可以fulfill物理去改變這個世界，對。所有模態融合在一起，這些模態是相互benefits，還是相互制約的？在過去一年，我們都一直在研究把這些模態的融合，我們從各個不同的研究裡面得到一些線索，在Cosmos 3的時候把它結合在一起。比如說在Cosmos Policy那篇work裡面，我們發覺把video跟action合在一起是幫助action的。當你在做World action model的時候，我們發覺除了predict你要做什麼action，你也predict接下來take到action之後會產生的states，也就是observation，這是幫助你做訓練或收斂。 所以我們知道predict那個video是幫助action的。聲音也可以幫助audio，聲音也可以幫助video generation，當你接觸到那個點，你就知道接觸的時間是那個點，所以知道這是有互補的。一般做video的人都知道，從文字到video這是一個訊息量變大的過程。訊息量變大代表說有很多種可能性，很多種可能性的function是比較難去學習的。就是一種東西可能有十種可能，這東西沒有固定的target不好學。所以在做video或image，大家都會發覺當你的那個文字描述非常詳盡的時候，是比較容易產生好的video。所以你知道就是文字的詳盡描述也可以幫這個video。 我們做這個Cosmos 3，其實是想把之前這些Cosmos Reason、Cosmos Predict、Cosmos Transfer、Cosmos Policy這些東西合在一起，合在一起本身就有它的好處，整個東西都簡化了。這些訊號怎麼合在一起讓他產生互補的作用，而不是互相抗拒的作用？這是可以經由實驗的設計、經由這個data的比例的調整來達到的。所以我們那時候是堅信著，這些訊號都是描述同一個世界，這些訊號都含有這個視覺怎麼運作的資訊，所以應該有種方式當他們合在一起會互相幫助，更多訊息幫助你了解這個背後的運作原理是什麼。所以有這樣的believe，從這個方向出發吧。 所以這這裡面有什麼secret sauce嗎，在互相benefit上？其實做大模型沒有什麼secret sauce，做嚴格的實驗、假設實驗、看到結果、累積知識，在做下一個實驗，就一步一步往下走，走扎實的做就對了。我不覺得有什麼不可告人的秘密，你要追求的東西就是耐心、做實驗、驗證，對了下一步，就這樣。 因為你們這次採取的是一個雙塔的設計，一個塔在處理離散資訊，一個塔在處理連續資訊，為什麼採取這個設計？有很多個原因，一開始的時候Cosmos Reason就是離散的，從那個vision language model出發，那predict transfer policy是連續的，所以我們知道離散的work for understanding，我們知道連續的work for generation，把它放在一起就是一個現階段很合理的選擇。有很多種用Cosmos的方法，但常用的是做post training，就是也許用你的video action的pair去train generator，或者你想只用Reason，就是用你當自己domain的這些understanding data來train。當你有兩個這樣可以合在一起，可以把gradient的影響切開的，是幫助客戶去使用的。 如果我們全部連在一起，那generation跟understanding就在一起了。現在要換這個generation的distribution，在換的過程中因為全部都在一起，你的data也會改變你understanding那邊的表現。如果你沒有兩個tower，它是合在一起的話，那understanding這邊既然要改變，但你的data又沒有understanding的data，它很容易understanding部分就丟失了。那就不方便客戶使用，所以分成兩個，對使用上是有一定的幫助的，雖然它相比起來還是不夠elegant，因為你還是要事先決定它的架構。在整個deep learning就是讓data來決定怎麼做，而不是你人為去區分，但是在這個階段，我覺得這個東西是比較合理、比較適合使用的。講到這個東西大家也可以猜出來，我們下一步目標是更加簡單一點，為什麼要有兩個tower，一個tower就夠了。但我們在往一個tower動做目標前進的時候，我們要考慮到這些使用者是不是容易用上它，因為我們不是我們的目標是幫助大家贏，而不是自己產生一個可以做很多事情的模型。我們相信機器人的每個都不太一樣，有個相機的數量、相機的位置、自動駕駛每個都不太一樣，我們需要客製。我們要機器人合在一起去追求這個工程的簡約完美，我們要考慮到使用者是不是有辦法去利用它，這是我們必須研究的課題。 但是相對來說離散的訊號更好處理，把融合進了連續的變量之後，它的處理難度會變高，你們怎麼合在一起並且能夠把它scale上去呢？對，現在有一系列的work像Transformer，它是可以同時處理離散跟連續的。在做Next token prediction的時候它是做離散的，然後在做diffusion是連續的，那是在做同個tower，這段時間有很多種不同的變異，大家在研究這些大家都關切的問題。這些訊號可以用離散表示也可以用連續表示，我希望開發這種架構是全部離散或者全部連續，讓事情相對變得更簡單一點。但這是一個理想，也不確定能不能成功，也要做一步一步的實驗驗證往下去走。 這裡面看離散更容易還是連續更容易啊？各自有各自的好處，離散比較好做訓練，然後連續它有它的漂亮的地方。最關鍵的就是做客戶還是要求部署，部署的時候一些成本的考量都很重要。我覺得最後會根據部署是最適合什麼樣的模型，我覺得是最容易獲得成功，所以有很多方向要積極在自己考慮。 有一個研究員問你，因為你現在是把所有的模態都訓練到一個模型裡去，那理論上它也可以coding做得很好，也可以讓機器能運作，然後也能夠生成我們這種創作者的內容，那它有可能在這單一維度，比如說coding上比Claude還要強嗎？我還是喜歡講孫子兵法講的背多者立。對，如果你什麼都要好的話，你在訓練過程當中，你要博學多文，你要觸摸很多不同的類別，讓你提升你的知識水平。但當你確定你只需要用這個單一的application的時候，是不是有些調整是因為你不care其他東西的？有沒有東西是你願意放棄，然後就是做比較好？如果你什麼都不願意放棄的話，你就會搭得很辛苦。我覺得策略往往就是關乎著你決定你可以放棄什麼東西。 這雙塔之間會互相的提升嗎？我們在這個pebble模型實驗就是說，當你用更接近physical AI的reasoner的話，可以幫你做更好的physical AI的generation。然後我們也知道如果你可以把generator的gradient傳回這個reasoner的話，理論上是可以幫助你的reasoner做更好的representation學習。在這個paper裡面受限於時間，我們並沒有做成完整我們想做的實驗，已經很多實驗了，但是 foundation model開發本身就是一個決定，兩三個月前就做的，很難快速去做到一些調整。我們不把這個3看成是一個終極，我們會繼續做3.1、3.2，一步一步往下走，把一些我們想做進去的能力繼續補下去。 除了可能會更簡單，雙塔有可能會變成一個塔，還會有什麼在未來模型中可能會看到的這些變化？我們很關注這個模型怎麼去幫助那個Physical AI的builder。泛化是一個核心的問題，任何AI的核心問題。在物理世界的AI裡面，我們需要達到泛化，就是假設我今天教機器說，我的衣服我喜歡這樣折，喜歡這樣放，我就給這個機器看一兩次我怎麼做，它是不是就可以快速學會在現實生活中？然後是不是給這個Physical AI讀了說明書，他就知道怎麼去操作這個儀器？我覺得這些重要的能力是Physical AI能達到的。我想說在foundation model部分是有什麼東西我們能做的，來幫助這些Physical AI的developer，所以我們中間的提升可能都是會朝着泛化能力的角度去把這些東西加進Cosmos模型裡面。 優化是像加速那些是一定會發生的，我們當時希望這個模型可以越跑越快。然後另外一些優化就是能力的優化、泛化的優化，那這個東西就是可能改一些架構上、或者一些training的objective的改變，或者用一些特殊的curated data來做這個訓練。不是很清楚，需要測試。 對，是實驗科學，我常常覺得那個Deep learning就很像中醫一樣，是試出來的。在試的過程當中你做詳細的實驗記錄，從裡面去理解那個中間的法則，我覺得這是很有趣的。我做嚴格的實驗、控制變數，然後得到一些關鍵的結果，是這模型疊代的重要步驟。 有一個研究員提出，這個模型的語言訓練是不是可以把它的比重加得更大一些，因為他們還是認為LLM是提升智能的關鍵。你認嗎？我覺得在早期的image generation研究，包含Google的Imagine裡面就提出了，當你的language的understanding越強，你用更大的T5 model，你的image generation能力越強。我是相信的，你用更大的semantic representation應該幫助，我是同意的。畢竟Physical AI的應用跟大家常講的coding或Chat還是不太一樣。所以還是需要做一些實驗驗證，我想做的事情是了解真正的痛點，從這痛點裡面去找到合適的方式來把這個模型能力補強。當裡面沒有資源的限制的時候你當然會放進去，但比較有挑戰的是當你資源有限的時候，你怎麼做取捨。 你需要你的機器能會算數問題嗎？你需要你的機器能會解這個coding的問題嗎？我覺得你可能更關注他能不能把你的工廠要組裝的東西做好，或者家庭的衛生把這些操作把它做好，對吧？所以我覺得你要解的問題似乎是跟這些大語言模型公司、Agent公司要解決的問題是不太一樣，所以既然是不同的題目，最後解這個題目的成本都是一個重要的考量。那在這些條件下它的架構真的會跟現有的這些Agent的模型是一樣的嗎？這個是我一直在思考的問題。 縱觀人類的歷史答案就是應該是不同的，你要做耕田的工具跟你要做紡織的工具是不一樣的。這些工具是不一樣的，最後就是背多者立，是吧？然後我主張的智慧還是蠻管用的。 Cosmos你們未來會投入多少卡和資源？我們一直在提升當中，不知道方便講這個卡的數量，但我們希望我們已經在萬級了，然後希望可以更多更多。 Nvidia有數個模型項目，我們主要是把它分成兩類。一個叫Agentic AI，就是要去做coding、Chat，這些digital intelligent的客戶需求；在Physical AI這邊主要就是Cosmos這個模型。Cosmos是一個base model，Nvidia也很關注自動駕駛，也很關注機器人，也關注工廠，對我們而言這些都是Physical AI的應用。在自動駕駛上面我們就有Alpha，他是再Cosmos上面做的specialization，讓他們做自動駕駛所需要做的這些優化。還有Gust是在Cosmos上面做對機器人相關的優化，還有包含了這些後body control的這些東西。我是把這些做自動駕駛、做機器人看成是我的客戶，希望幫助他們成功，我們就是以Cosmos為核心來去輔助這些主要的開發者可以獲得成功。你說是是不是最重要的呢？這兩個都很重要，很難講誰比較重要。但現在打的最火熱的就是這些Agentic AI的東西，那Physical AI是下一步。 這應該是一個巨大的市場，我估這個市值不是我的專業，也不方便代表公司講多大，但是隨著人口的老化，隨著對製造業的自主的需求，各式各樣的應用，我們覺得這將會是一個巨大的市場。 一般來講，模型的評測有三步。第一步就是benchmark，有一些固定的dataset去上面量測它的quality；第二種是arena style，就是A跟B比看誰比較好；第三種就是跟著expert，去真正有這個痛點的人去解這個問題。我覺得這三個都很重要，在Physical AI裡面第三個特別重要。因為每個機器人的東西不太一樣，那Physical AI每個要解決的問題也不太一樣。 我覺得它是會相互benefit，在過去一年之間，我們都一直在研究把這些模態的融合，在Cosmos 3的時候把它結合在一起。那這些訊號怎麼合在一起讓他產生互補的作用？這是可以經由實驗的設計、經由這個data的比例的調整來達到的。我們堅信著這些訊號都是描述同一個世界，這些訊號都含有這個視覺怎麼運作的資訊，所以應該有種方式當他們合在一起會互相幫助。從這個方向出發吧。 其實做大模型沒有什麼secret sauce，做嚴格的實驗、假設實驗、看到結果、累積知識，在做下一個實驗，就一步一步往下走，走扎實的做就對了。我不覺得有什麼不可告人的秘密，你要追求的東西就是耐心、做實驗、驗證，對了下一步，就這種。 那時候聽到97代，我的理解就是我們有決心就是一路把它做下去把它做好。這是一個很大的commitment，因為這個做真模型是很耗費資源的。然後它展現出他的決心就是往下走，我得到的信心就是這個東西會一直往下走。那世界模型最後就是接近這個世界，從透過那個模型你可以去學習你想在真正世界裡面學習的東西。那我覺得這是往那個方向走，最接近的就是你有能力隨時可以做出一個黑客帝國的劇場。那我我不確定是不是需要到97代，因為我們如果就是每半年一代的話，還得40幾年對吧？我覺得既然想追求更好的結果，時間有限，好處就是我們繼續做3.1、3.2，會把之前沒做好的東西再把它補進去。 大概是40幾塊，也許不需要到40幾年。目前在Nemo那邊，他們有一個叫做Hybrid Architecture，就是有些層是傳統的Transformer，但有些層是Mom-bus style的接近RNN的這種架構。因為RNN的運算在inference是比這個Transformer便宜的，然後agent有一堆token要產生，所以這是運算成本的需求。那Physical AI這邊我們的架構不太走這個路線，還是走比較傳統的Transformer，但我們走到Mitchel Transformer，把這個action加進去。所以這兩邊的模型發展是漸漸有點不太一樣的。我們兩個團隊之間緊密合作，你可以從Cosmos 3的paper發覺，很多作者是從Nemo來的，我們就是跟他們合作吸取他們的經驗。在未來這兩模型有可能統一升一個更大的模型嗎？這都是有可能，我們的決策很簡單，怎麼樣可以幫助客戶最好。我們是很low ego的。 我印象中論文好像有290多人，這是一個組織架構的難題，你希望所有的目標一致，但你也希望所有人可以自主做決定。當全部人的目標一致，但每個人都不能做決定，進展會很緩慢。如果大家都可以做決定，但大家目標不一致，就會發散。所以跑這麼大一個Project重要就是讓大家的目標一致，但每個人又可以做出決定。在組織架構上面，我花了很多心思去設計一些團隊運作的合作方式，讓資訊透明化。當然我們也提供了容錯的空間，當一個人害怕他的決定錯誤的時候，他是不會做決定的。 大家都清楚整個Project最後要達到目標是什麼，你要一直去把未來給畫清楚，跟每個人講。在Cosmos 3 paper出來之前或者做完之前，我就跟主要的幾個leader還有各大部分的團隊都講過我們做出來的模型會是這樣子。當大家都看到同一個願景的時候就比較容易集中，就像籃球選手在發球之前你都會想像這個球空心落網。 我是一個很講究團結合作的人，我們的會很多，一週大概44有吧，我想應該超過10次。我們做Video的定期開會，做Understanding也是定期，還有各各環節的，就是會蠻多的。儘量做的一件事情就是讓每個會議都更加engaging，讓每個人都能參與，那這都是需要訓練的。永遠不會最高效，你可以大家都不開會各做各的，那不一定會成功；你可以一直開會，什麼都不做只是講，也不會成功。你就要選一個均衡。 我們是都有，天下分久必合，合久必分。我覺得都是要掌握在那時間點了解什麼是對這個團隊最正確的事情，而不是一味的順著一個模式操作。 我覺得Physical AI和機器人的爆發可能會是正相關，我們都在追求Physical AI的ChatGPT moment。ChatGPT moment是什麼呢？就是讓大家清楚看到了這樣的scaling，這樣一個好的模型對人的生活會產生什麼樣的變化。ChatGPT moment for Physical AI應該是指大家看到一個以AI為主的Physical AI應用，讓大家清楚看到說這東西是真的有用。這必然會發生，但是我不知道是什麼時候。 我覺得人形機器人它的優勢就是可以利用大量的人類數據，來獲得學習的素材，提升效果。但人形機器人本身就是越像人有腿有手，相對的複雜度也比較高。很多人在做輪式的，只有上半身是人，下半身是一種移動平台，這也可以解決很多問題。我覺得最終全人形是最適合各式各樣的操作，因為人類的環境就是設計給全人形的，但最後關鍵還是營運成本。 我希望早一點，但我不知道，我覺得它必然會發生。 我用黑客帝國裡的幫大家具象。就是有一個世界模型怎麼Create去加速學習，很多人擔心就是AI能力太強的時候會take over the world，把我們Eliminate。我覺得這個社會是人去建立的，這些科技是人在背後操作，只要大家是有共識，我覺得這件事是不會發生的。科技可以大幅度地改善人類的生活，我們也需要做出這些相應的Guidelines來確保它不會去影響正常世界發展。 我們不是不會把Cosmos和Nemo的這一層跟CUDA做同等的連接，因為現在的整個發展已經相對複雜很多，有很多個不同的Stack合在一起，我覺得合在一起可能是新的CUDA。 CUDA一開始的時候就是在尋找Application，後來在Deep Learning出來之後，找到最重要的Application。複製CUDA是一個很好的想法，但我不敢斷定你可以按照原來的路線走一樣的路，環境是不一樣的，所以我覺得是要Adaptive，要看清楚哪個時間點可以做出哪些重要的貢獻。我們不會拿著這個錘子去造釘子，就像老黃常講的，他的Decision是基於First Principle，他是覺得什麼合理就往那個方向走。 這是很巨大的變化。公司從股價來看是有起起伏伏的，當初TPA出來的時候公司掉了一大截，有些同事們對公司未來產生了一些想法就離職了，後來我們每年在Perf裡面都擊敗了TPI，產生出更好的性價比。在長期間我更理解到什麼叫慢慢經營，找一個你相信的目標。 我就想做科研，GPU是做科研重要的工具，我要去一個GPU眾多的地方，而不去一個生產GPU的地方。 他承受的壓力大了很多，但他還是關心員工，很仔細地去閱讀公司Top 5 Email，找到一些訊號幫助整個公司往前進。變化是很大的，那時候沒有Physical AI，現在Physical AI都成為了重點。 他每天都這樣提醒自己，要做出對公司最正確的決定。Pandemic的時候很多公司開始裁員，我們決定裁的就是Travel，員工就不用去Travel全部線上，這樣就省下一筆錢。你可以看到公司就是在困難的時機，做出一些相對不平凡的決定，想的是什麼可以節省掉一些不必要的開銷，照顧員工，一起度過困難。 裁員有很多好處，但大部分公司都不是這麼做的。Nvidia是一個想長久經營下去的公司，他看到這麼多的風風雨雨，選擇這個東西一定有他的道理。 我不喜歡賽馬。我們通常都是take這個mission，我們相信你，給你很高的目標，希望你完成，用老黃話講就是讓你接受Pain and Suffering，torture you to greatness。 做這些大模型，這幾年都是這麼辛苦地在往下走。很多都是從錯誤中學習。自从GPT起來之後，整個AI從業人員大家都有莫名的壓力，迭代速度快，揮灑GPU資源和年輕人的時間。 我很高效，早上四到五點起來。生活步調都變快，還是要學東西，處理的事情變得更多，找讓自己更加高效。 很多都是可以在Nvidia工作的時候就可以感受到的，他認識很多員工。Mission is the boss，而不是你的Boss是你的Boss。那Top Five讓公司各個地方的微小訊號都可以擴散出去。 大家都是很聰明的，但如果人跟人之間無法互信的話，也很難達到1加1大於2。大家都防備對方，不願意把最好的一面展現出來，那這樣子也不見得是一個好的狀態。我覺得多元是一個比較好的狀態。 這是一種Confidence，是一種自信。我的目標是幫助大家做好，但我對自己做東西要求很高。 這是我受他影響非常深的地方。年輕的時候如果是一個研究員，你的目標就是讓論文在頂級會議被接受，你要Stay of Art，代表你要擊敗所有的人。但我站的位置不一樣了，成為管理者之後，我希望培養新一代的人才，站在Nvidia這個Ecosystem的角色，幫助他們成長。 不管是做員工還是外部合作伙伴，這是一種真實的想幫助他們。我們是Design House，最後我們也沒有什麼Consumer product，都是靠著其他公司幫我們做GPU卡。大家一起成長，不能說錢只有我們賺，我們要跟整個Ecosystem一起成長。 六邊形戰士。不管是在電腦架構的Register細節，還是怎麼做Marketing、怎麼說High Level的東西，他在處理定價、客戶關係，他面面俱到。這是極大的熱情，He gives everything he has to help the company be successful。 目標就是Torture everybody to greatness。通過這些磨練讓你更加理解怎麼樣做才能做出不平凡的事情。這很耗精力的，你要花時間去Torture別人，這不是容易的事情。 「Are you okay, baby? 這世界上沒有什麼事是公平的。」這句話讓我知道找理由是沒有用的，就是應該Give everything you have，讓我印象深刻。 我覺得科学的发展本身就是incremental的，你是随时随地都可以提出improvement，做出對這個世界貢獻最大的事情。 我覺得我我很開心，10年前我在講這個Deep Learning會取代Computer Graphics成為一個最重要的影像生成模式，這件事情發生了。我的目標是Physics AI的成功，這是我下一個目標。 如果我對這個模型很驕傲，我在我的能力範圍內，我做出了我自己滿意的東西，那我很接受。外部的很多衡量指標現在慢慢都沒有那麼重要了，幫助別人夠不夠多才是重點。 這兩邊我也覺得都做得很好，而且在中國我們看到這些公司都招收很多實習生，這對人類整個知識的Diffusion是一件好事。 這東西既然都在這裡了，人自然會想下一步是什麼。並沒有覺得有什麼催眠的事情，只是對現在的時間而言，做LLM是一件很正確的事情。 我覺得社會組織只要還是好的良性狀態，就會不斷有創新發生。 我覺得模型的能力慢慢就會統一，領先的模型有更充裕的時間去找到這個區分的方式，強大的公司也會打造自己強大的Ecosystem。沒有理由模型會不一樣，它會Diffused。 算力慢慢就會起來，抱負有決心去挪動資源建大模型團隊的人很多。這是一個自然的平衡狀態。 因為中國強大的製造業，讓產生一個很優越的條件，可以讓這些公司做一些在美國不容易做到的事情。 怎樣從這個有限的訊號裡面學到需要的操作，應用於一個沒有看過的場景。這就是Physic AI裡面要解的主要問題。 如果五年後我們可以清楚講出來ChatGPT Moment是什麼時候發生，是在哪裡發生，我覺得這志願是可以明確講出來的。 我希望別人講Cosmos吧，希望Cosmos可能是一個重要的推手，幫助這些重要的Physic AI builder達到這個ChatGPT Moment。 不要緊張，對自己有信心，看清楚什麼地方值得你投入精力。不要被宣傳而覺得路越走越窄。 用語言來描述這世界上發生有趣的事。 我覺得不需要把別人殺死，我們的心態是我們怎麼去造市場。CUDA就是造市場，我們想的是怎麼造出下一個大的市場。 他經常說這句話。我們就是要做這種Zero billion dollar的 business，因為成功後會變成billion。 他會Work，I cannot，這世界上沒有絕對，做AI本身就是很有趣的一件事情。看到貢獻改變世界，我覺得是很有成就感的事情。

Rahm Emanuel: Trump's Foreign Policy, China, Europe's Decline, Immigration & DSA vs Democrats

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·71:36·48.1K views·2026-08-13
Rahm EmanuelUS Ambassador to Japan Jason CalacanisHost, All-In Podcast David FriedbergHost, All-In Podcast
TL;DRRahm Emanuel outlines a strategic roadmap for the United States—focusing on economic blocks to isolate China, aggressive workforce and education overhauls, and a centrist political coalition—while forcefully distinguishing mainstream Democrats from the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA).

TL;DR

Rahm Emanuel outlines a strategic roadmap for the United States—focusing on economic blocks to isolate China, aggressive workforce and education overhauls, and a centrist political coalition—while forcefully distinguishing mainstream Democrats from the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA).

Leadership, History, and Foreign Policy Strategy

  • Comparing Past Presidencies: Rahm Emanuel noted that Bill Clinton integrated policy, politics, and press in three-dimensional chess, achieving a net-positive government perception and signing welfare reform. President Obama inherited the Great Recession, steered the nation away from depression, ended two wars, and repositioned America as a global force for good.
  • The "Summit of Disruption" & China: Emanuel warned that the world's two superpowers are exporting their domestic dysfunction. China's primary export is economic dysfunction (crushing industrial bases globally with excess WTO cases), while the US exports political and cultural polarization.
  • Economic Block Strategy: Rather than unilateral tariffs, Emanuel advocates forming a robust economic block—aggregating Australia, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan, the EU, and Latin American allies—to isolate China and rebuild Western manufacturing and technological resilience.
  • Middle East Dynamics: Emanuel highlighted three regional trends: Abraham Accords allies (UAE, Bahrain) staying aligned with the US, hedging nations (Saudi Arabia, Egypt, Jordan, Pakistan, Turkey, Qatar), and the severe degradation of Iranian proxies (Hamas degraded, Hezbollah cornered, Syria talking security with Israel).

Defense, Taiwan, and Global Alliances

  • Taiwan & South China Sea Realities: Emanuel stressed that kinetic force to change the status quo in Taiwan is unacceptable, and the US must prepare for multiple Chinese playbooks: a 96-hour invasion defense, an economic quarantine (requiring reinforced posture in Okinawa and the Philippines), and incremental island seizures.
  • Japan as the Strategic Anchor: Japan is the long pole in America’s Indo-Pacific policy, hosting our largest single-country military footprint and a permanently based aircraft carrier. Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi's public statement that a move on Taiwan is a national security threat has drawn severe Chinese economic and military coercion, requiring stronger US backing.
  • Critique of Trump’s Foreign Policy Style: Emanuel criticized President Trump for punching down at middle-power allies (like Brazil, Germany, or Italy) while trying to appease strongmen like Putin and Xi, whom Emanuel argues view Trump as a joke rather than an equal.

Domestic Economy, National Debt, and Taxation

  • Fiscal Recklessness & The Debt: Emanuel argued that the US national debt ballooned because the Bush administration committed the historic error of fighting two wars (Iraq and Afghanistan) paired with two major tax cuts. He stated that solving the $38T+ deficit requires capping spending growth for 5 years alongside tax code reform.
  • Tax Code Overhaul: He lambasted the current tax code for rewarding wealth preservation and generational inheritance (such as the step-by-step basis) rather than wealth creation.
  • Minimum Wage & Labor: Drawing on his experience as mayor of Chicago and working with Bill Clinton, Emanuel endorsed raising the federal minimum wage coupled with a robust Earned Income Tax Credit (EITC) to ensure work pays without increasing unemployment.

Immigration and Education Reform

  • Immigration Solution (The Dignity Act): Emanuel endorsed the *Dignity Act* (backed by 23 House Republicans and 23 Democrats), which satisfies the principles of being a nation of laws and a nation of immigrants by securing the border, penalizing illegal entry, and securing green cards for foreign graduates to retain top global talent.
  • Education & Higher Ed Overhaul: Citing his success raising Chicago high school graduation rates from 56% to 83%, Emanuel outlined a sweeping plan: universal full-day Pre-K, rigorous elementary accountability, revamping high school with dual-enrollment college credits (targeting a 3-year BA), making community college free, and allowing universities to take up to 20% full-pay foreign students to subsidize American affordability.

The Democratic Party vs. The DSA

  • Sharp Split with the Left: Emanuel drew a hard line against the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), stating they are a separate party masquerading as Democrats ("I'll see you in November on the ballot").
  • Strategic Divide: He rejected the DSA's assertion that mainstream Democrats (like Obama, Pelosi, and Hakeim Jeffries) are an existential crisis, arguing instead that Donald Trump is the true existential crisis and that progressive purity tests do nothing to achieve practical goals like raising the minimum wage or expanding healthcare.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Education Targets (Rahm Emanuel): Target for 100% of high school students to graduate with college credits through dual enrollment and community college partnerships.
  • Higher Education Cost Cap (Rahm Emanuel): Proposal that families earning under $200,000 should attend college for free, and tuition for incomes above $200,000 should be legally capped so it cannot rise faster than inflation.

Notable Quotes

*"You can't have progressive policies if people think that the government can't run and organize a one-car parade."* — Rahm Emanuel

>

*"China wants everybody dependent on China and them independent of you... Our markets are going to be so big and important you can't do anything about it. We're going to leverage that to then destroy, manipulate, and control your sovereignty."* — Rahm Emanuel

>

*"This president punches down and kisses up. He has this fixation about Putin and Xi admiring him as an equal and they think he's a joke... He is nothing but a bully and nobody's buying it anymore."* — Rahm Emanuel
All right, everybody. We are super pleased to have Rahm Emanuel join us for the All-In interview today. He was the senior adviser to Bill Clinton, the last president to preside over zero national debt, and he was Obama's chief of staff, mayor of Shytown, and ambassador to my favorite country to visit, Japan, under President Biden. He hasn't made an official announcement about his presidential run in 2028, nor do I think he's going to make an announcement here, but he's been talking a lot about where the country needs to go in a post-Trump world, specifically in 2028. Freeberg and I are going to ask Rahm about the most important topics facing America today: the economy, inflation, employment, surging national debt, never-ending wars, the rise of socialism, and sadly, anti-semitism, immigration—skilled and legal—and how his positions have evolved over four decades of service to America. Welcome to the All-In Interview. Rahm Emanuel, welcome, Rahm. I'm—yeah. Okay, there you go. I didn't know that was the cue. I was waiting for that; it felt like an obituary. I was waiting for the rabbi to get up. That introduction is the only one that ever starts with Clinton. Usually, they start with my career in Congress, so I give you a lot of kudos for remembering that I started as senior adviser to President Clinton. Well, let's start with that. What did you learn under Clinton and Obama? And you know, these two—what I would say are the last two sane presidents we seem to have had who were moderates. In my mind, I'm a moderate, slightly left-leaning, but these seem to have been two great presidential runs that you were involved in. What did you learn from each of those individuals? They have different skill sets, but they had an outlook of the world. They had an outlook of the United States in the world. They had an outlook of kind of a progressive, affirmative view of government. I think, most importantly, which I kind of remind everybody: you can't have progressive policies if people think that the government can't run and organize a one-car parade. Not possible. They have to have an affirmative view. And the last time you've had a net positive was under Bill Clinton. It actually wasn't just the balanced budget that helped, but it shifts when he signs welfare reform, and you can go back and look at the data. Bill Clinton was a unique person who, in my view, was able to integrate in three-dimensional chess both policy, politics, and press. He understood the presidency in a way that Teddy Roosevelt had redefined it; he was redefining it for the 21st century and he really meant what he said about being a bridge. And nobody was better, not just at giving a speech, but taking a policy and being able to communicate to carry people from where they were to change, to not make change threatening, and then achieve the objectives you wanted. That said, President Obama, in my own view, coming at the end—or not the end, but the Iraq war, etc.—understood that this was a different presidency. Both the end of the Iraq war and the financial meltdown meant that we were at a pivotal, transitional moment for America. And I do think, look, every president gets judged on what world they inherited and what world they leave behind. And sometimes the harshest critics of both Obama and Clinton come from the left, not the right. And I said, "Is it the peace or the prosperity you don't like most?" Because I'll address it. Was it the fact that post-Reagan, Clinton's economy grew the most, jobs grew the most, and income disparity shrunk the most? And people were calling it the "unipolar" moment where no country had the power the United States had economically, politically, and militarily. Now we can get back to what happened there. And then, in the same way, the left criticizes Obama for being kind of tepid. He inherits a country that's near a depression—known as the Great Recession—but he saves it from that. He transitions from losing 800,000 jobs to creating 200,000 jobs, and he gets America out of two wars. He continues the war on terror, obviously, but repositions America in the world to be a force for good. And I learned how you—it's not like a to-do kit. There's no to-do kit in the drawer of the Resolute desk, but the merging of policy, politics, and communications is key. Did Obama export American democracy as a point of primary policy? And did that lead to this world that we find ourselves in today where there's this pullback, because a lot of what gets exported ends up being kind of one side of the political spectrum, not the other? And now we're kind of seeing this: what did we do? I made this point a long time ago. I said it recently at the Aspen Security Conference, and I'm now writing my next op-ed on this as a prelude to the summit between Xi and Trump. I'm labeling it the "summit of disruption." Xi's largest export is not autos or electronics; it's the domestic economic dysfunction of China. They're crushing everybody else's manufacturing base across the world. They have more WTO cases against China than any other country, and all of them combined are against China—Chilean steel plants, Indonesian garment plants, Brazil's iron furnaces. We are exporting our domestic political and cultural dysfunction. That is, the two superpowers are holding the world hostage. One through economic dysfunction and internal issues—I mean, Xi refuses to deal with the real estate bubble popping, a consumer that's flat on their back, and an industrial base that makes no rational sense. Literally, their auto industry is now exporting more cars while their profits are down 20%. Through Trump, you can look at what he's doing in Brazil, meeting with the neo-Nazi party (the AfD) nine days before the German election. There were race tensions in Great Britain; we seem to want to exacerbate them. We are literally exporting both political and cultural dysfunction from our home onto the world. Now, I happen to think this president stylistically likes to punch down and kiss up to Putin and Xi, but that's a stylistic point. But that's what the two superpowers are doing; they don't seem to have managed their internal affairs, and now they're exporting it to the rest of the world as a dumping ground for their domestic problems. I happen to think, coming back from Japan, that rather than have tariffs, we should have an economic block so all of us together can withstand China—to actually organize that economic block and meet them in the marketplace. That's how we win friends and influence enemies. Is that a different view than the Obama-era notion that we should be exporting American culture—American democracy, I think was the framing—and that then opened the floodgates to exporting our culture wars? And then our culture wars became this kind of battleground where there's like transgender theater in Africa and things like that? No, I don't think that's what opened the door. Look, David, I think the United States of America, like every country, has four tools in their national security toolbox: military power, political persuasion, economic statecraft, and cultural attraction. And I think America's culture at large, not just Hollywood—the American brand—is a cultural attraction, or was. I actually think this president has hurt the American brand, our economic statecraft, our market, our technology, our universities and research. Our national labs are a piece of our economic statecraft, a piece of our national security and our ability. You know, there was a great story the other day by David Graham: gentlemen, only the United States could get Russia and Ukraine around the table. There is no other world power. Our political persuasion is an asset in our national security toolbox. If you wanted to do what I laid out in my speech in the Middle East in Israel—the 23-state solution—China, Russia, or Europe cannot do that. Only we can do that. Now, we have massive political-military power, but I happen to think it's not prepared for the future. That's on both parties; I was present for one of them. I think we got so enamored with the war on terror that we lost sight of the transformation that was happening on the battlefield and the technologies. You can see that in the drone area, as an example. Neither Iran nor Ukraine have a navy, and they both control waterways near their fronts. That tells you everything you need to know: you're not fighting two wars, you're fighting two different types of wars, and we've got to get ready for that. Reed Hastings on Life After Netflix and the Next Wave of AI | The CEO Signal

Semafor(29.9K)·47:36·173 views·2026-08-13
Reed HastingsCo-founder and Former CEO, Netflix Penny PritzkerHost, The CEO Signal Andrew Edgecliffe-JohnsonHost, The CEO Signal
TL;DRReed Hastings reflects on his transition out of Netflix after 29 years, his new role turning around Powder Mountain in Utah, and why he views the exponential growth of AI as a workforce-doubling opportunity for companies willing to embrace creative destruction.
Planned...

TL;DR

Reed Hastings reflects on his transition out of Netflix after 29 years, his new role turning around Powder Mountain in Utah, and why he views the exponential growth of AI as a workforce-doubling opportunity for companies willing to embrace creative destruction.

Netflix Succession & Leadership Culture

  • Planned Succession: Hastings stepped down as CEO in January 2023 (three and a half years prior to the interview) and recently left the board, describing board membership as being "like being a godparent versus a parent."
      • He promoted Ted Sarandos to co-CEO in 2020 during COVID-19, and later promoted Greg Peters to co-CEO two and a half years before leaving, noting that they operated effectively as co-CEOs before it was formalized.
  • Values Tension: Hastings emphasized that corporate culture involves managing inherent tensions, such as balancing honesty versus kindness, stating that Netflix explicitly chose to "lean into honesty" even when it feels unkind in a professional context.
  • Championship Sports Team vs. Family: Netflix deliberately rejected the "family" metaphor for companies because it discourages necessary layoffs, opting instead for a "championship sports team" model where managers make roster changes to win.
  • Farming for Dissent: To combat groupthink among respected peers, leadership explicitly names dissent-seeking exercises, uses negative-10 to positive-10 scoring on decisions, and encourages the "informed captain" to gather diverse viewpoints before making a final call.

Powder Mountain: The Second Act

  • The Turnaround Project: After retiring from Netflix and spending a ski season at Powder Mountain, Hastings bought the losing-money resort in Utah to fix it up, describing it as a physical, local, and boutique counter-balance to his career of global virtual scale.
  • Resort Business Model: The 10,000-acre property (comparable in size to Manhattan) combines a private ski club model (capped at 650 households, similar to the Yellowstone Club) alongside a public ski area where Hastings invested heavily in three new lifts, snowmaking, and new lodges to appease locals.
  • Executive Motivation: Hastings chose to take an executive CEO role rather than remain a passive investor because "any CEO would tell you they love running things," viewing Powder Mountain as a passion project and community-building endeavor with profitability as a secondary marker of success.

AI, Creative Destruction, and Corporate Strategy

  • Exponential Improvement: Hastings noted that AI has been getting roughly twice as good every year for the last decade, meaning it will be 32 times smarter in 5 years and 1,000 times better in 10 years.
  • The Workforce Dividend: CEOs should treat AI advances as the equivalent of doubling their workforce overnight—giving them the choice to cut headcount in half or use the surplus resources to attack competitors.
  • Judgment Over Paranoia: Challenging the adage "only the paranoid survive," Hastings argued that paranoia leads to delusions about low-probability events (like a comet striking), asserting that leaders instead need sharp *judgment* to navigate real threats and opportunities.
  • Anthropic Perspective: As an Anthropic board member, Hastings praised the team's "humanist" mission to deploy AI safely for humanity, while acknowledging the challenge of competing against rapidly moving frontier labs.

Philanthropy & Education Technology

  • Charter Schools and Innovation: Hastings championed charter schools as independent, non-profit public schools that can operate more efficiently and effectively than traditional systems.
  • Personalized Learning via AI: Predicting that AI will eventually revolutionize K-12 education by allowing children to learn at their own pace instead of rigid grade-level tracks, Hastings compared its future impact to how chess was transformed by AI tutoring, which created more active, skilled players than ever before.
  • Changing Teacher Roles: As AI takes over factual transmission and tutoring, teachers will be freed up to focus on emotional regulation, communication, teamwork, and human judgment.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI Improvement Rate: AI will continue its exponential trajectory, becoming 32 times smarter and more effective in 5 years, and 1,000 times better in 10 years — Reed Hastings.
  • Education Breakthrough: AI and technology will enable students to learn continuously at their own individual pace rather than being locked into rigid grade-level curriculums within the next couple of years — Reed Hastings.

Notable Quotes

"When you're a parent, you're on the front line. It's like that's being CEO. And so then you step off of that and it life changes a lot. The board when things are going well... is a pretty easy gig and a relaxed form. I would say it's like being a godparent versus a parent." — Reed Hastings
"For the last decade, AI has been getting roughly twice as good every year... in 5 years, the AI will be 32 times smarter, more effective than it is today. You asked, what do CEOs have to do? You know, it's like doubling your workforce." — Reed Hastings
"Only the paranoid survive in some sense... but taken literally, the paranoid have delusions. They're delusional. And if you spend all your time worrying about things that are very, very unlikely to happen... then that's not time focused on scenarios that matter more." — Reed Hastings
