Not left a hole for you? No. I still talk with them socially and we hang out some, but the dramatic one is going from 70 hours to one hour of work. That's the drama. Has it all been social or have you been tempted to call up Ted and Greg about business matters too? No, I've been very disciplined on that. And the good news is for the first couple months I would lob in some things and they were clear that they knew, you know, we haven't worked together that they are able to say "what would Reed think," and then they sort of put that aside and do what they think is best, which is what they should do, right? So they can mimic me and conjure me easily. I'm very predictable in that way. So, you're the first CEO that we've had on this show who's left a giant job and started a second act. Tell us how you're spending your time now. Yeah, so much fun. I've been a lifetime skier, very enjoying it, but not much time to do it. I retired from Netflix in January, went to our ski house, which is in Powder Mountain, but again, just a customer, a kind of one-acre customer. I spent the rest of the winter skiing because suddenly I'm not in business meetings. At the end of that season, I decided to buy the mountain and to fix it up. It's 10,000 acres, which is almost as big as the island of Manhattan. It's quite large. It's a glorious resort that needed some work and was losing money. It's still losing money, but now we're investing a ton: new lifts and a big private luxury real estate development, a lot like Yellowstone Club, but like an artistic younger version of the Yellowstone Club. Before we go into that in more detail, let me ask you, you've led a transition. When did it go from succession planning at Netflix to you thinking about you and what you were going to spend your time on? I wasn't planning on Powder. So I would say I finished Netflix in January 2023, handed that over, and then spending the winter skiing was unwinding and reflecting. As it turns out, I got sucked into a different model, which is to really try something very physical compared to something virtual, very local rather than very global, and sort of knowing all your customers as opposed to Netflix, which is vast. Was that a deliberate decision to react because you'd spent a life being global, virtual, and dealing with millions of customers? Were you sort of reacting to that and looking for something that was absolutely different? Can we take you back to the beginning of the succession story that led up to this new chapter? Yeah. It was 2020, middle of COVID, you appointed Ted Sarandos as your co-CEO, and at the time I remember reading you saying you were looking forward to the next 10 years at Netflix. What changed? I think COVID probably changed a lot of things for me. I didn't get particularly sick. I didn't lose anyone really close to me, but I definitely felt like, "wow, life can have some twists and turns." I realized I could maybe stay at Netflix for 10 more years, but I think my life—having spent 25 years doing it every day—could just be different and more interesting. I knew Ted and Greg were fully capable of running it. It's not that it was going to run worse; it probably would run better. So those combinations really brought it home for me. Your exit from Netflix was not abrupt, but rather feels more like a process than a moment to me. You made Ted your co-CEO in 2020 and Greg was the COO then, and two and a half years later you made them co-CEOs. So, you promoted Greg, really stayed the same? Exactly. Became co-CEOs. Yeah, that's right. So what did you see during that period that convinced you the right succession was for Ted and Greg to lead together rather than let's say Ted, who had been your co-CEO, just take over? Yeah, I would say there was nothing hugely specific. It was the general—they're working together more and more, getting used to that. It was just a bunch of very positive interactions and also some difficult moments where we were working through things, and that's what builds the bonds that just made me confident that I could move forward and that they would pick up the mantle. So as a journalist, I've covered quite a lot of co-CEO arrangements and they don't always go smoothly. I've seen stories where one co-CEO falls out with the other or where parts of the organization are trying to work around one co-CEO to get an answer they prefer from the other. What have you learned from being a co-CEO yourself and then handing off to two co-CEOs about making that relationship work? I don't try to pitch it as a general model. I don't run around saying, "Oh, we've discovered something. You should always have co-CEOs." I think of it as a high-performance technique. If you've got two people who really trust each other and who love the organization more than themselves, then you can get some real benefit because they've got two people who can be at different places at different times. It works really well, but again, it's a rare set of personalities to make it work so well, which we were fortunate to have. Did they push you to say it's time for us to be the co-CEOs and you to be chairman? Were you pushing them that you ought to come closer? You two seem to lead well together. Kind of how was that dynamic? They weren't explicitly pushing in any way, but I was ending up giving them more and more discretion. They were effectively operating as co-CEOs for a while before I formalized it by promoting Greg into my position. I'm curious who makes the final call in a co-CEO structure. I'm thinking of the big call Netflix had to make this year to walk away from the Warner Brothers Discovery deal that you looked at for a while. Was that Ted? Was that Greg? Was that ultimately you as chair? I wasn't involved in that call. So, it was them together, sorting through it—here are the tradeoffs and coming to that decision on its own. We would have stayed in at the same price, right? But the bid went up; it was really a question of whether to raise the bid or not. So it was value price rather than strategy. Yeah, again, we were very comfortable with the strategy and we were comfortable with the price that we had paid or had offered to pay. We were very excited about doing the deal. Then, you know, capitalism is a great system, and so another bidder, Larry Ellison, raised the bid and paid Warner Brothers even more. Then we had a chance to match. It's all very clean and we decided not to match. When did you know they weren't going to need you and your judgment anymore? I would say that was a phenomenon of growing confidence. In other words, probably five years ago or 10 years ago, I was like, "I think that's going to be true." It becomes more and more true over time. But I would say through COVID, we were all stressed and they made just tremendously good decisions together. So I would say that's the real proving ground. And did it take some effort to get your board to get comfortable with this structure or did they just say, "Oh, I see it and it's obvious"? No, they were very involved and we very much contemplated the standard alternatives. What if it's just one of them? What if it's an outsider? What if you don't go? I mean, you know, all these different elements, so they participated throughout that process and were good counsel and independent views. So you built Netflix around a very distinct set of leadership principles, which are so fascinating. I think you've written a lot about this: radical candor, the keeper test, giving people context rather than control. So looking back, where did that culture really work and where did you have to course correct? We wrote a whole book about that, *No Rules Rules*, to help other entrepreneurs build upon it and improve upon it. I benefited so much from all the literature before I was trying to lead, and this was a give-back to go through all that. That all came together organically over 10 or 15 years. When we first started in 1997 or 1998, we had no specific culture really. We were just asking, "Are we going to survive?" Then there was the 2000 internet slowdown. We did do a big layoff. That was the first surprise and lesson: after the layoff, we were like, "Wow, we're actually getting more done, not less, even though we laid off a third of the company." We would have never done it proactively. We tried to figure out why, and of course, the answer was talent density, which is doing the best you can to hold on to the two-thirds that were going to most help you. We realized, wow, maybe we should do a one-third layoff every year. Of course, that's impractical, but we said, how about if we keep the bar high and really think about us as a championship sports team rather than a family? So year by year, these kinds of ideas came in. And then in terms of management, the context-not-control, the informed captain, and the farming for dissent—all of these shorthand phrases came in over time as we worked through the system. Did you find the company's receptiveness to that set of values changed over time as people got more acclimatized to them? Sure. Employees want to understand change, and you want to frame it as an alternate and often superior set of values rather than an abandoning of good values. So let's take the tension between kindness and honesty. What do we want in our kids? We want them to be kind and we want them to be honest, but when they're very young they say, "Why is that lady fat?" and then we teach them about that tension. We teach them to be a little less honest and more kind. Okay, so that's a values tension. Same thing in the workplace, but in the workplace, we want you to be very engaged with feedback to help us all grow in our performance. So we explicitly say lean into honesty, and it will feel unkind, but it really is the right thing to do in this professional context. You have to frame it as there's a values tension. We're explicitly choosing a different place on this continuum and here's why. Team over family. Both are good metaphors. People respect great teams and they respect families and how we operate. But if you describe yourself as a family at a company, you better not ever do a layoff. You would never lay off two of your kids, right? Then people get very cynical if you say it's a family but don't operate that way. If you say it's like a championship sports team and we've got all these competitors and we want to win the championship, then people understand why the coaches make changes throughout the year to do their best to win the championship. Can I dig into a concept that is really interesting to me, the idea of farming for dissent? It's really hard to get a group of people who respect each other to actively seek out disagreement. How'd you do that? How'd you make that work? How'd you make that real at Netflix? Well, I would say the top-level way is naming it. So, calling it "farming for dissent," then we're clearly trying to generate it. Then there's a bunch of tactics underneath that of asking, "What are the other opinions?" Then we would do exercises if we're trying to make a decision. We talk about leaders being an informed captain. How do they get informed? They might poll all their people and we would score it on a -10 (disaster) to 0 (I'm not sure) to 10 (this is the best thing we could ever do) scale. We'd have everybody say their view and why. It wasn't a democracy. You might get a whole bunch of negative threes and still decide to do it, but you wanted to be informed. So those were exercises in farming for dissent and then we could have great fun years later going back and looking at the decision and who had been for or against it, whether that was global expansion or what show we're going to do next. What did you do with that hindsight? Specifically, it was just to get you to think more carefully about the future, because you can't undo those decisions. Each person had a chance to analyze what they guessed wrong and how to move that forward. Can you give us an example of when dissent helped change your mind? Well, when you say "change my mind," that implies that I wanted to do X. Think of it more like we're exploring something, and in particular, it's important for the leader to keep an open mind. You're not farming for dissent if you say, "We are definitely going to do this show, I just want to hear any contra opinions." There's no point to that. That's right. So, I would start it off and be like, "I think this show that we're doing in Latin America is new and fresh, but I'm worried that the downside is we get in a huge fight with all the traditionalists." So, a little framing of reasonable arguments for both sides, and then we would go at it—discussing the particular team, the casting, and comparisons to other series in the market. That would be the typical discussion. Netflix is moving on from its founder era with your departure from the board. How do you think that's going to change it? Well, it's got to change it a lot. If they're going to improve the firm, they've got to not try to relive how I did things. They've got to be able to find their own models and that comes with making their own mistakes. It's just continuing to grow the firm and to earn more of consumers' business and time. So, I'm not sure in what specific ways. That's kind of the joy of it; it's them being able to express themselves and their ideas. You've gone from leading in rough numbers what, 15,000 people to I think when you started at Powder, it was about 150. That's right. Totally different order of magnitude. How much of the Netflix leadership model applies at Powder? Oh, the vast majority of it. I mean, skiing also has a safety culture. Around safety issues, you don't want "freedom and responsibility." Like lift safety and maintenance, there are some things that are pretty highly regulated. But outside of that, in terms of empowering people, in terms of moving the decision-making down, in terms of farming for dissent, all those things have worked very well. Powder has an unusual business model. Can you talk us through how this mountain works? It's actually not that unusual in general. There are 4,000 private golf clubs in the US, but in skiing there's only three. It's partially because you need a lot of acres, but it's basically the same model: you've got a clubhouse, a membership, you get better tee times, a restaurant, social atmosphere. Everyone who's a member of a golf club gets it. What if that was applied to skiing and it was much less crowded and you knew a bunch of the people and it was friendlier? So that's private skiing. Now, we also have public skiing and that's working well too; that's on a different side of the mountain. Curious that you decide to go in as CEO. You could have been just an investor or an adviser. What made you think, "No, actually, I want to take an executive role here"? Well, any CEO would tell you they love running things. It's having meetings, making decisions—all of that is really exciting. Is this a passion project or a real business? It's a passion project. The big goal is to build a community that is so enticing that Patty and I want to spend a third of our time up there. There's just so much vitality. Second is to really build a place of beauty that's about the art and the architecture, so that you just have an aesthetic "wow." And third is that it makes a bunch of money. I like capitalism; it's a good marker. I think it's going to be very successful, but that's number three. So I'm very curious that you've decided to focus all your energies on a single mountain. Not just because of this pendulum swing from the scale chasing that you did at Netflix, but because so much of the ski industry now seems to be about the multi-mountain pass. It's about trying to get everyone access to everything in a single company's orbit. Why have you gone the other way from a company like Vail Resorts? Well, I think Epic and Ikon, the two big multipasses, are great innovations and they're potentially strong businesses. Think of them like Costco. They're sort of mass market. It's great value and that gets more people skiing, and so we love that. But we're trying to be super boutique, high-end, luxury, special. Frankly, they're the most important business and we're kind of the most beautiful one or the most enjoyable one, with no compromises, just incredible skiing limited to the 600 families. And are the locals who've been skiing on Powder for 50 years reacting well to the idea that some of it's more exclusive? How is that being received? You know, there's tension. It's inevitable. But we've invested a lot in the public ski area, adding three new lifts, adding snowmaking, adding new lodges. So, we're really trying to improve the public side, and they see that. They don't love it, but it's not a big tension point. Welcome to the hospitality industry. There we are. Got to get our hotel up. Trying to figure out what should be the signature hotel at the top of Powder Mountain. It is quite interesting because at Netflix you were bringing affordable entertainment to the masses, and here you're doing something very different where half this mountain is reserved for 650 households. What's your response to the people who don't love it? This is one path that mountain could have gone because it was in financial trouble. It was going to change no matter what, and it didn't go the way of just becoming a classic, very crowded resort. So it's a turnaround for you. Oh, very much so. If it was running fine, I probably would have stayed a customer and just had my house there. But living there and seeing the needs it had and the opportunity, that's what really pulled me in. It's the people who are in the community, the 200 families that I love and have gotten to know, and they're all curious and happy and healthy and successful. It's been just a really fantastic community. The CEO Signal is presented by PWC. AI is delivering efficiency, but it's also creating something else: a workforce dividend. As AI frees up human capacity, expertise, and time across your organization, that dividend begins to emerge. The question becomes what to do with this surplus. Realize the immediate profits and reduce spending, or invest them in future growth? Treating AI solely as a cost opportunity risks trading tomorrow's growth for today's margins. The real leadership decision is what to build with the value AI creates. How leaders use their workforce dividend today may shape their long-term advantage. I want to shift to another part of your portfolio career post-Netflix. You sit on the Anthropic board and there's quite a strong through-line here. You studied AI as I understand it when you were in college. I know, crazy. 1986 to 1988 I got a degree in AI. What were you writing about it then? AI of the time was writing down specific facts or inferences or rules. Ultimately those systems never worked; they were way too rigid, way too specific, and didn't encode how we think. Then 40 years later, neural nets are now big enough to be incredibly profound and interesting, but there was no way to do that in the mid-1980s. So we were just way, way too early. The new techniques have worked very well and we have incredible, improving intelligence from all the frontier labs. We're beginning a new phase of humanity where we're not the most intelligent creatures. Today you are generally more intelligent than AI about a field you really know, but when you ask it about lots of other fields that you don't specifically know, it's very helpful. People say it's like a graduate student in a whole lot of areas, but not yet a PhD, but it is improving. So, Reed, sitting on the board of Anthropic, you get to see an extraordinary amount of what's going on. How has that affected your attitude? The Anthropic team is very committed to the mission of bringing AI about in a beneficial way for humanity, and that's their North Star more than the biggest capital stock or the biggest company. At their heart, they're more humanist than capitalist. The challenges that they face are many because all of their competitors are moving very quickly, but they're very incredible human beings and also very good cognitive scientists. What do you think corporate CEOs are still underestimating about AI or misunderstanding about how it's going to change their companies? For the last decade, AI has been getting roughly twice as good every year. If that continues—which looks pretty likely—then in five years the AI will be 32 times smarter and more effective than it is today. Think about it: you're using one of the frontier models today and it's pretty impressive. Think of it 30 times better just in five years. Then in 10 years, that's a thousand times better. What do CEOs have to do? It's like doubling your workforce. Suddenly, you've got double your workforce. You could either cut your workforce in half and stay in the same business lines, or you can use all these new resources to expand against your competitors. You're going to see a lot of companies attacking each other, which is the great act of creative destruction. It's gotten easier and easier to make software, and soon it'll be easier to make everything. You've lived through some of the most important waves of creative disruption in the media and technology industries. When you look at this moment in AI, do you think most CEOs are still underestimating how much disruption is coming to traditional business models? I think most humans are underestimating what "doubling every year"—that exponential rate of improvement—is going to do. We don't have many examples. Cars didn't get better at that pace. Computer chips did, but they were sort of buried a little bit as one part of the economy. It's very rare for things to be improving exponentially. There's no guarantee that it will continue, but it certainly looks like it because there's so much focus on it. What's the CEO's role in guiding AI policy within a company? The CEO's role is to keep an open lens around the right things. It's easy to say "only the paranoid survive." In some sense that makes a point, but taken literally, the paranoid have delusions. If you spend all your time worrying about things that are very, very unlikely to happen, like a comet hitting the planet, that's not time focused on scenarios that matter more. Only the paranoid survive is way too simplistic. It's really the people who have judgment that survive. Which cases, which threats, which opportunities—how do you sort through them? It's sort of like a master chess player: some scenarios they go very deep, other scenarios not so deep. If you get that wrong, you lose. That's the art of the strategy side of the CEO. Is that also a point about the risk of focusing too much on the downside potential and not paying enough attention to how this technology can unleash new opportunities for a company? Both are important, but often we don't have that much control over some of these scenarios. You can worry about whether it will destabilize monetary policy, but if it does, you'll just have to cope with it. You really need to focus on your customers and the things under your control. There's a lot of positives to the huge productivity that we're all experiencing. Let me take you to your new life here. You've gone from this very online world of streaming to this very offline world of skiing. How much are you using AI yourself as you get to grips with these new industries like real estate and new technologies like ski lifts? All the time. I'm asking AI to teach me how ski lifts work, how sewer systems work, what property developments have worked and why. So I'm the student for so much of the AI session, and then we're using AI to improve the software in the place. Our application for moving around and buying things—integrating the front end and back end—is all so much easier to do now because of AI. Everywhere we can, we're using AI, as is everyone around the world, which is why the revenue of those frontier labs is growing so fast. I've heard many CEOs say big companies have the resources to truly invest in AI and get the most out of it. It's going to be harder for smaller companies to match that. Having gone from the resources of Netflix to the resources of Powder, do you see that disparity? Do you think you are a little on the back foot against the Epics and the Ikons in terms of your ability to deploy the technology? No, I think sometimes the small companies are the most innovative and will be the most aggressive users of AI to unlock various scenarios to compete with their competitors. It varies quite a bit, so I think it's more attitudinal than the size of the firm. It certainly depends on the applicability of it. Let me ask you something on a totally different subject. We share a passion for K-12 education and its importance in society. You served as president of the California State Board of Education, you've championed charter schools, and you're on the board of Khan Academy. What do you think your personal impact has been there beyond being philanthropically active and providing financial support? Any great society needs a great education system that provides as many kids as possible the opportunity to flourish, and we're making some real progress. Charter schools are non-profit public schools that are independently run. They can be more efficient, more effective, and they are much of the time. That's been one big wave of improvement and reform. The second one is technology: how can technology be well done for schools and students? We don't really have any amazing positive results yet. People sometimes associate social networking and negative things with technology in schools, but honestly, using computers in schools makes a lot of sense. We have to figure out how to do it well. The big breakthrough to come is letting kids learn at their own pace. Today we've got a very regimented system—fourth grade, fifth grade, sixth grade—and a lot of kids are behind and feel dumb, while some kids are ahead and feel bored. It's super hard as a teacher. It would be so much better if kids could learn at their own pace. That's what technology is going to be able to allow over the next couple of years. We just let the notions of the fifth-grade curriculum become less important and the notions of kids flourishing and being excited to learn new things continue to grow. I'm optimistic that technology and AI will have a really positive effect. But again, the current technology doesn't yet deliver on that promise. We're getting closer, but we need the AI to be more engaging, more thoughtful, more safe. But again, back to the doubling rate of improvement: AI is getting better and better, so it's going to crack this one. Isn't there a valid question about whether you want young brains to be turning to AI to learn how to think? Let's look at chess. Chess was solved by computers 30 years ago. Instead of abandoning chess, there are more active chess players than ever before. The typical 10-year-old, 15-year-old, or 20-year-old is significantly better than those 30 years ago. Why? Because they're getting AI chess tutoring. On average, AI chess tutoring is much better than your uncle. It's really opened up the field. I hope we can do that for biology, politics, chemistry, and math. I don't want to rush into it—maybe in five years we're ready—but I really think it's coming. AI will first be much better than the typical teacher, then much better than the very best, then much better than all teachers. It can be patient, it can be steady, and it can follow you your whole life. It's all kinds of positives. But Reed, don't you think with effective and responsible AI in a classroom, the role of the teacher changes? Exactly. As opposed to being a transmitter of facts, I could be much more engaging with you in judgments about what to do with a set of facts, how to work with other people, emotional regulation, and all kinds of important things not limited to the curriculum. Really important stuff like emotions, communication, and teamwork—that's what teachers will be able to focus on. But that's not what they were trained to do. That's not what they went through teacher school for. It's still a big change. These are big policy questions, and you're a relatively unusual CEO in that you were a meaningful political donor even while you were running Netflix, at a time when a lot of CEOs have been running away from anything that looks remotely political. Now that you're free of the big job and you're on a mountain in Utah, are we going to see more political activity from you? Not particularly. I'm a supporter of politicians who are steady and have good ideas. I've supported some Republicans, more Democrats, but I'm not a core partisan. I don't define my life that way. I mostly disagree with Trump, but when he does something I agree with, I try to make a point of expressing that. I think we should take these things issue by issue and try to seek what's the best possible country. Can I ask you just to wrap up finally for us? We've talked about individual pieces of this portfolio career that you've built for yourself post-Netflix, from Powder to philanthropy to your board responsibilities in AI. Is there a thread that ties them all together? The only thread I can think of is that I love taking risks. I love challenging myself and all the things that we're doing are really hard, whether that's education, Powder Mountain, AI, or the work before that. There's something very stimulating about big challenges where you really might fail, and then working really hard to avoid that and to hopefully have a big success. I always say it's a privilege to work on hard problems. Reed Hastings, thank you so much. This has really been a pleasure to learn from you and to hear your story. Thank you guys. Thanks for joining us. Thank you. Stay with us. Penny and I will be back after the break with the readout. Are you ready to bust some silos? It is time to get on the same page. Zero full stack mesh, dynamic collaborative ecosystem. 1 + 1 equals 3. Left hand meets right hand. Break down silos. At PWC, we don't just talk tech. We make yours work harder together. Great plan. Let's do it. We'll get back so you can become a more intelligent enterprise. So Penny, I thought that was fascinating, not least because of how deliberate he's been in choosing to do something that's the exact opposite of what his life at Netflix was. He's gone from the ultimate mass-scale digital experience where you're selling to millions of customers you never see, to this very physical, real-life world where he's focusing on a very local community and you can literally walk down the street and bump into every one of your customers. Absolutely. I think the word "deliberate" comes through for me about him. He's had a very successful succession planning effort, but it's been incredibly intentional and it took quite a long time. He really grew Ted and Greg to be the leaders that they are, and then brought one in as his co-CEO, brought the second in as co-CEO. He also recognized that his role had to change as he went from being CEO to being chairman. He talks about that as like going from being the parent to the godparent of your company. I love that. I love that analysis. Ultimately, the test of this succession process will be whether anybody makes a Netflix drama about the succession at Netflix or not. But I think it is an extension of the leadership culture he tried to foster while he was CEO and then moving on to the board. I hear a lot of CEOs talk about values. I'd never heard one talk about "values tension" there. He talks about the need to be honest versus the need to be kind and what's the balance. He also talked about the need for what kind of team you put together. Is it a family? He prefers one that's more akin to a championship sports team where you're really culling for the best of the best. With that "values tension" phrase, he's saying he's willing to put up with a little bit of friction and trade that for higher performance. Then we had a fascinating part of the conversation about AI. I suspected, given his 29 years in a very disruptive company and then his role in Anthropic, that he'd have strong views on this. He gave his fellow CEOs a couple of very clear ideas about what their role is to lead through this particular change. That's about balancing responsibility with curiosity or open-mindedness. He really leans in on open-mindedness. In the end, he thinks AI is the ultimate disruptor and that you as a CEO need to really be on your front foot thinking about what's coming at you. Because he's talking about the power of this technology doubling every year, you think about that—technology being 32 times more powerful in 5 years' time. That's within your planning cycle. The message I took away for his fellow CEOs is that some of you are going to end up being Blockbuster if you don't get on the right side of this, because other people are busy building the next great disruptive Netflix. Absolutely. It was fantastic. Thanks, Penny. Thank you, Edge. And for more conversations like this, don't forget to follow the CEO Signal wherever you get your podcast and like and subscribe.

SK Group Chairman Chey Tae-won on SK Hynix's massive memory expansion — full interview

CNBC Television(3.4M)·32:44·5.5K views·2026-08-13
Chey Tae-wonChairman, SK Group Katie TarasovReporter, CNBC
TL;DRSK Group Chairman Chey Tae-won explains that AI's explosive growth is driving massive demand for memory, prompting SK Hynix to invest heavily in fab expansions—including a $720 billion buildout plan—despite severe near-term supply constraints and high "chipflation.
" AI as a...

TL;DR

SK Group Chairman Chey Tae-won explains that AI's explosive growth is driving massive demand for memory, prompting SK Hynix to invest heavily in fab expansions—including a $720 billion buildout plan—despite severe near-term supply constraints and high "chipflation."

AI Demand and the Memory Super-Cycle

  • AI as a Growing Child: Chey Tae-won likens current AI to a four-year-old child that will require exponentially more memory, knowledge, and experience as it matures into an adult.
  • Explosive Volume Forecasts: Chey Tae-won projects that AI agents will scale 77-fold within five years, driving total memory production capacity demands up nearly five times within ten years.
  • Balanced Conglomerate Portfolio: Chey Tae-won notes that while SK Hynix is currently the most important subsidiary, SK Group's broader portfolio strategically incorporates energy and telecommunication companies to support future AI data centers.

Supply Bottlenecks, "Chipflation," and Customers

  • Lead Times and Shortages: Chey Tae-won states that expanding manufacturing capacity requires a 4-to-5-year lead time, leading to a severe market crunch where customers are requesting double their previous volume.
  • The Hardest Year Ahead: Chey Tae-won warns that next year (following the interview) will be the worst year for memory shortages because memory companies are not yet prepared to match surging demand.
  • Rising Prices (Chipflation): Chey Tae-won acknowledges that memory prices have jumped 40% to 50%, forcing downstream consumer electronics, PC manufacturers, automakers, and companies like Apple to raise prices for society.
  • Key Partnerships and Customers: Chey Tae-won identifies Nvidia and Jensen Huang as their most important customer and partner in building the AI ecosystem, while also maintaining major relationships with hyperscalers (Google, Microsoft, Meta) and TSMC CEO C.C. Wei.

Managing Expansion Risk and Supply Constraints

  • Mitigating Overbuilding Risks: Chey Tae-won explains that SK Hynix avoids historical memory-bust overbuilding cycles by strictly verifying that demand exists before expanding, utilizing long-term agreements, joint venture fabs, and "memory as a service" risk-sharing models.
  • HBM Production Trade-offs: Chey Tae-won notes that producing High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) consumes more than four times the wafer space of normal DRAM, heavily eating into total capacity.
  • Token Cost Target: Chey Tae-won states that SK Hynix's ultimate target is lowering the token cost for AI, which he currently views as too high.

Global Footprint and Fab Expansion Strategy

  • Prerequisites for Fab Locations: Chey Tae-won stresses that building a memory fab requires water, power, land, and an underlying ecosystem of 600 to 700 supporting material, chemical, and service companies.
  • Global Footprint Evaluation: Chey Tae-won confirms that study teams are actively evaluating potential locations globally—including the U.S. (such as their Indiana project) and other regions—to address memory shortages based on where the right resources and ecosystem align.
  • Customer vs. Government Pressure: Chey Tae-won notes that while U.S. customers strongly desire fabs built on U.S. soil, he has not yet spoken directly with the government, driving decisions primarily through business demand.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Chey Tae-won predicts that AI agent usage will grow 77-fold within five years compared to 2025.
  • Chey Tae-won predicts that total memory volume demand will increase to nearly five times current production capacity within ten years.
  • Chey Tae-won claims that next year will be the hardest and worst year for memory and chip shortages.

Trade, tech and geopolitics: what you need to understand about China today

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·39:27·1.2K views·2026-08-13
Qian LiuFounder, Wusawa Ravi VelloorHost, Asian Insider
TL;DRDespite its economic miracle, China faces severe domestic challenges—including a depressed property market, youth unemployment rates as high as 46%, and weak consumer confidence—while simultaneously driving a massive export surge that is sparking…
international trade frictions...

TL;DR

Despite its economic miracle, China faces severe domestic challenges—including a depressed property market, youth unemployment rates as high as 46%, and weak consumer confidence—while simultaneously driving a massive export surge that is sparking international trade frictions with both Europe and Southeast Asia.

1. State of the Chinese Economy: Growth, Consumption, and Real Estate

  • Diminishing Reliance on Exports:
    • Historic Peak vs. Present: At its peak in 2007, exports accounted for 35% of China's GDP; last year, that figure dropped to 18–19%.
    • US Exposure: Exports to the US represent less than 10% of total Chinese exports, meaning an absolute collapse of US trade would impact China’s GDP by only about 2%.
    • Surplus Growth: Despite declining as a percentage of GDP, China's trade surplus grew by 20% year-over-year, hitting $1.2 trillion last year—producing the economic equivalent of a new country the size of Switzerland or Saudi Arabia annually.
  • The Private Consumption Crisis (40% of GDP):
    • Slower Income Growth: Annual disposable income growth has slowed to 5% (matching the 5% GDP growth rate), down from previous double-digit (8%+) levels.
    • High Unemployment: General unemployment is high, and youth unemployment has hit extreme levels, with surveys (including professorial estimates) recording figures as high as 46% a couple of years ago.
    • Wealth Effect and Property: Over 90% of Chinese households own a home, and 20% own two or more. Falling property prices have erased perceived household wealth, severely denting consumer confidence and spending.
  • Investment and Institutional Sentiment:
    • The "Venture Capital" Analogy: China historically functioned like Silicon Valley—provinces and cities competed like pipeline projects, encouraging a "fail fast, pivot fast" mindset.
    • Risk-Averse Shift: The current KPI across many sectors has shifted from innovation/pivoting to "making fewer mistakes," resulting in the safest bet being to do nothing at all.
  • Property Market Outlook:
    • Long-Term Pessimism: While urbanization and income growth provide minor support, structural drags (oversupply in certain regions, demographic shifts) mean real estate will not return as a fast-growing economic engine.
    • Niche Pockets: Growth opportunities remain in specific segments, such as elder-care real estate for Australia-based and local developers.

2. Trade Friction and Geopolitics: Europe, Southeast Asia, and Rare Earths

  • Impending Trade War with Europe:
    • Escalation Dynamics: While US-China trade friction involves targeted tariffs and tech bans under a transactional "art of the deal" framework, European sentiment is hardening into structural discontent over trade deficits and domestic unemployment.
    • Targeted Retaliation:
      • Europe imposed tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles (EVs)—a sector representing less than 1% of total Europe-China trade.
      • China retaliated with surgical precision against sectors representing less than 1% of trade, targeting European agriculture and French cognac (specifically from the Cognac region).
  • Impact on Southeast Asia (ASEAN):
    • Displaced Industries: The export push—driven by domestic manufacturing capacity and weak local consumption—is undercutting regional manufacturing, causing significant losses in Indonesia's textile industry, Malaysia's furniture sector, and Thailand's auto industry.
    • The Ice Cream Analogy: As Premier Li Chang noted, China rejects the "second China shock" label, framing its manufacturing output as a competitive, high-quality, low-cost offering ("selling cheap vanilla ice cream") rather than a forced imposition.
  • Rare Earth Dominance:
    • Strategic Restraint: China did not want to deploy its rare-earth dominance card, noting that leaders like Deng Xiaoping identified rare earths 40 years ago ("The Middle East has oil, China has rare earth"), yet Beijing refrained from using it across multiple US administrations.
    • Trigger Point: China finally played the rare-earth card only after tariff negotiations with the US dragged on with unacceptably high risks.

3. Global Leadership, Supply Chains, and Industry Shifts

  • US-China Dynamics in 2026:
    • Distracted US: US involvement in global flashpoints (Venezuela/Maduro, Greenland, Iran, Russia-Ukraine, Israel-Palestine) has left Washington's attention and trade bandwidth heavily constrained regarding China.
    • Standoff Stability: President Trump's Beijing visit and praise of President Xi followed China's rare-earth show of force. While bilateral relations will remain stable before the US midterms, fundamental rivalry across technology, military security, and finance continues unabated.
  • Emerging Sectors:
    • Following historic waves in EVs, AI (DeepSeek), and robotics, the next major global push from China is projected to be biotech (exemplified by Boston-trained founders moving R&D centers back to China while retaining US sales offices).
  • The Strait of Hormuz Effect:
    • Limited Domestic Impact: Less than 50% of China's oil imports pass through the Strait of Hormuz, and oil accounts for only 6–7% of China's total energy use due to massive electrification and renewable energy adoption.
    • Reserves: China maintains an oil storage buffer of roughly 7 months, insulating citizens on the street from immediate shocks.

4. Gender Economics and Human Capital

  • The Rise of Female Power: Ranked alongside the rise of China as a defining theme of the century, female empowerment is central to economic productivity.
  • Return on Human Capital:
    • Financial vs. Human Investment: While US stocks/bonds returned an annual average of 2.4% over 46 years, each additional year of general education yields an average return of 8.8%.
    • The Female Dividend: The return on investment specifically for women's education is 10%. Furthermore, a wife's additional year of education increases her husband's earnings by 18% to 23%.
  • Macroeconomic Yield: According to UN Women data, investing in women can increase global GDP by $4 trillion USD by 2030 (over 3% of current global GDP).
  • Redefining Equality: True gender equality means equal access and opportunities—allowing women to work or men to stay home based entirely on personal choice, dismantling traditional rigidities (such as historical Shandong dining and marriage stigmas against female PhD holders).

Notable Quotes

"When we think about Chinese manufacturing think about China is a shop that sells ice creams... Imagine vanilla ice creams. And as you said, China feels like is so good... And so cheap... And that you buy so much out of China and you get fat. And then you complain it to China. And you say it's China's fault that you're becoming so fat. And China's like, 'Wait a second. You have every right not to buy us.'" — Qian Liu
"Europe is slowly cooking that negative sentiment about trade with China... once they do, it will stick. And when they do that, China will also retaliate." — Qian Liu
"China did not want to play the rare earth card. If it wanted to, it would have played this long time ago... Chairman Deng Xiaoping said decades ago that the Middle East has oil and China has rare earth. This is no secret." — Qian Liu
"Investing into human capital gives you a lot higher yield than financial capital. And within this 8.8% the returns to women education is 10%... when the wife's education grows by an additional year, the husband makes an additional somewhere between 18% to 23%." — Qian Liu
