More than 90% of Chinese households own a house, and 20% of them own at least two houses or even more. When property prices start to drop, that means all of a sudden I don't feel as rich as before. When the price was going up before, every day, even if I wasn't doing anything, I was becoming richer. Of course, I had the confidence to spend, but now I don't. When you put all these three together—I'm not earning as rapidly as before, I have a higher risk of getting unemployed, and I'm not as rich as before—obviously, the confidence to spend is much lower. That is really the biggest challenge for the private consumption part. Finally, there is the investment side. Whether it's infrastructure, the property market, or just overall fiscal investment, we are seeing a level of uncertainty, whether caused by the external world or the lack of confidence, feeding into that sentiment. Interestingly, there are times I compare China with Silicon Valley. Silicon Valley is a place where we have venture capital and we try a lot of different projects. In China, it's the same: different provinces compete, different cities compete. It's like a giant venture capital. The beauty of venture capital is that you have so many pipeline projects at the same time, and the spirit is to "fail fast and pivot fast." That was, in essence, how China was. People were encouraged to try different things; it's fine if you make mistakes, you just try something else. But right now, the overall sentiment is not to make any mistakes. If you change the KPI from pivoting fast to making fewer mistakes, what is the safe bet? The safer bet is not to do anything. That to me is a big challenge, that sentiment change in China. So, yeah, that's where China is. Chen, you know, in April 2020, I wrote a column in the Straits Times called "The Risks of Putting All Your Eggs in the China Basket." One of the points I picked up in that column is the massive debt pile of China Evergrande. I said, "Look, this doesn't look sustainable." It reminds me of the early days of the Asian financial crisis and what happened to real estate. I've heard lately that a lot of that poison has left the system. Is there hope for a turnaround in Chinese property in the near future? I wouldn't put my money on a high return for the Chinese property market in the longer term. There are certain positive factors that will continue to drive the market, whether it's the urbanization rate or the growth in people's incomes, but there are also big factors dragging this down. For instance, whether the current stocks in certain regions are much higher compared to the actual demand, or if we look at major demographic shifts. So, the property market will not be the fast-growing engine for the Chinese economy going forward. Maybe there are pockets of it, because I was talking to a major Australian property developer and they were talking about going into the elder care segment as a possible growth opportunity in China. Robin, just for anyone who's not following Asia quite as closely as both of you, the Evergrande affair was this Chinese property development company that got into a lot of trouble because of debt. There was so much in your initial answer, I don't know what to come back to. The first follow-up question I thought of—you said the impending trade war with Europe. I'm often curious, what is a trade war? Is there a trade war with the US at the moment? There have certainly been trade battles. But why do you think there's an apparently inevitable trade war with Europe? I hope I'm wrong, but perhaps instead of saying "inevitable," I would say the very likely upcoming trade war with Europe, initiated by Europe. I just came back from Europe and I'm more convinced and worried than ever about that. We call it a trade war, or trade frictions with the United States; you're still seeing bans on rare earths or high-end chips, and you still see quite a level of tariffs on many products. So I would still call that a trade war. But with Europe, it is different. The US, and especially the Trump administration, is very "practical" about this. To President Trump, it is more a negotiation; it's his "Art of the Deal." Everything is about coming up with a number that he would be happy about, and he can change it from morning to evening. But not so much with Europe. The way I see it is that Europe is slowly cooking that negative sentiment about trade with China and trade deficits with China. It takes quite some time for Europe to decide on actions, but once they do, it will stick. When they do that, China will also retaliate. China cares more about the United States than any other country, unfortunately in this case, and so China would retaliate, and then chances are Europe would add on top of that. The narratives are just changing; before it was just about trade, and now it is changing into "people are getting unemployed because of China." There's part of me that almost hates to repeat this at the podcast because it only reinforces that. There are two of me at the moment: there is the economist me, advising the world that risks are coming, and there is the human me. We know that psychologically, the more we repeat something, the chances are it will happen, or the more you're scared about something, the chances are it will happen. This is where I'm worried. I had a line at the Economist about how China looks at Europe—people both love it and hate it. People love it because they believe it's so true, and they hate it because it's so brutal. I used to study and live in Europe, so there's part of me that doesn't want to say it. But the line was that Europe is being seen as an aging concubine that has not accepted the fact that it's been ditched by the American emperor. As someone who studied and lived there, Europe is like my second home. It pains me to say that, and I certainly do not hope that we will see that trade war between Europe and China. Is it like any war—a hot war—where you can see a buildup of arms and then someone goes over the top? Do you know when it's going to happen and what the first battle will be? Is it going to be about a certain product line, and is this going to happen in the next six months or in the next two years? Technically, it already happened. Europe imposed certain tariffs and barriers on, for instance, Chinese EV cars, and China, as retaliation, also took on certain things such as agriculture and cognac, for instance. It is so interesting because EVs are less than 1% of the trade between Europe and China, but they are becoming the dominant talk. China's retaliation is almost perfectly engineered to go against that. One of those was cognac, because it has to be cognac from the region of Cognac in France, which is so typically European, and it is also less than 1% of the total trade. So, China has engineered this beautifully. Technically it's already happened, but more is being discussed as we speak. Later this year, I think there's a very high likelihood that we're going to see more actions from Brussels. Sure. I know the Chinese also love their cognac, but have they tried Armagnac as a substitute? I'm not an expert on alcohol, so I can't differentiate the regions. Maybe the Armagnac producers should be lobbying Beijing for the cognac, right? Can I just ask you a question? You talked about trade and how Chinese trade as a factor of Chinese GDP has actually come down significantly. Yes. But we in Southeast Asia are very sensitive to China's economy. We want the Chinese economy to do well because it helps us as well. One of the issues we're having is that, look, you did mention that trade as a percentage of GDP has come down, but we're talking about a much bigger Chinese economy now. This morning, Premier Li Qiang said, "Please don't talk about the second China shock." The fact is that China has gotten so good at manufacturing, and maybe you don't have much of an option because, as you said, investment is slowing and consumption is taking a breather. The Chinese export push, especially after the US markets became a little constrained, is causing a lot of grief in Southeast Asia. In Indonesia, we have had significant losses in the textile industry, and the Chinese are selling shirts to Indonesia. In Malaysia, the furniture industry is in crisis because Chinese exports are so good. The Thai auto industry is in trouble. We remember China when we signed the ASEAN-China FTA; China gave us an "early harvest" which helped all the ASEAN economies. But today, because of your own domestic constraints, are you compelled to keep pushing the exports out into the world, including Europe? Do you need to find jobs for people? 45% youth unemployment is a frightening number. What do you think China can do so it doesn't kill the goose of consumption in the surrounding economies that are so critical to it? That's a really good question, and there are a few folds we can look at this through. To start with, when I say export doesn't matter to China that much, I meant from the Chinese economy perspective. But I certainly understand the level of concern the rest of the world would have. Even if 5% of GDP growth is what China enjoys every year, that is every year China produces a new country the size of Switzerland or Saudi Arabia. So that is enormous. Last year, the trade surplus was 1.2 trillion; that is absolutely enormous. So I understand this. Let me try to reframe it and explain how China looks at this. Think about China as a shop that sells ice creams—imagine vanilla ice creams. China feels that it is so good and so cheap, and you buy so much out of China that you get fat. Then you complain to China and say it's China's fault that you're becoming fat. China's like, "Wait a second, you have every right not to buy from us. You buy it not because we force it on you, but because we're so good and so cheap." So who is the one to blame? When we talk about trade surpluses and deficits, there's a part of China that feels a bit vulnerable, in the sense that we would love to buy more from you, but what do you have to sell to us? But we're back with trade wars, so governments could say, "Okay, it is great goods from China, but we're getting fat," to use your analogy. We'll put up trade barriers. Isn't that the inevitable result? Some people might say that, yes. But we need to get more time to be as fit as you are; that would be a healthier way. Protections are for the weak. Rather than saying, "I'm going to not buy your ice creams," you can also say, "I'm going to produce better ice creams that are tastier and cheaper." As trained economists, we know that tariffs are bad; protections are for the weak. To go back to your question about how sustainable this is, in fact, China itself realizes that this is not sustainable. When we talk about "you" in China—whether it's the government, the business, or the Chinese people—the answers are completely different. For all of the above, we know that it's not sustainable. For instance, when you look at the much younger people, there is this phenomenon called "lying flat," where the young generation believes that it's much more important to be happy rather than work hard and make money. I'm from the generation where, when I grew up, we had very little food; I couldn't have meat in my dumplings except at Chinese New Year. It's a completely different kind of mentality. When you are a lot better off, you don't bother to work as hard anymore. That is part of the reason why this is not going to be sustainable. On top of it, businesses realize this is not sustainable. When we visit some of the Chinese companies in different cities and regions, we see that right after COVID they were working extra hard even if they were losing money. I asked them why. They said, "I know I'm losing money, but if I don't, I would lose out even more money." But already, especially since last year, we're seeing that this so-called "involution"—the extra competitiveness among Chinese companies themselves—is starting to change. They are changing that organically. The Chinese government also realizes this; that is why the Prime Minister mentioned that the "China shock" is the wrong way to put it. The government has all this pressure from Europe and Latin America; they want China to buy more to reduce that trade deficit. China will look to do that. One way would be the service account; depending on how you want to play around with the numbers, the service account trade deficit that China runs increased by almost seven times in the last five years. I just wanted to mention the Strait of Hormuz's effect on China. I'm interested to hear from Ravi as well on the impact that's had in Southeast Asia. What impact has it had on China? I've spoken to several Chinese people who've said, "Actually, we've been pretty insulated from it because of the electrification of our cars and the fact that we're producing so much renewable energy." How do you see it, and do you think it's a game-changer? Economically, it hasn't impacted China that much; it's more on the political and geopolitical front. Less than 50% of China's imports of oil go through that strait, whether from Iran, Iraq, or Saudi Arabia. Oil is only about 6% or 7% of total Chinese energy use. The impact directly on China is much less compared to, for instance, Europe. China has an oil storage of about seven months or so, so if you ask citizens on the street, they feel a bit, but not so much. But more importantly, it's the idea that this is changing and giving the world a different perspective of the current Trump administration and how it is really disrupting and shaking people's faith in US leadership. China is watching it more enthusiastically. Since you spoke about US leadership, how much of a watershed has this year been in global affairs? You saw a very respectful, polite Donald Trump show up in Beijing. How much has the world changed for China this year? From the US geopolitical perspective, yes, so much has changed. We started the year seeing the US taking President Maduro from Venezuela, and then President Trump kept insisting and being even more assertive on Greenland, for instance. Then we had Iran, and then we had President Trump visiting China. Somewhat, to China, this year was more about watching the US destroying itself and its allies globally. You're absolutely right that President Trump visited China and he called the presidency a great leader and China incredible, but China is not naive. It doesn't believe this is how the bilateral relationship will be going forward. The very reason why President Trump turned his stance and style with China is because China showed the rare earth card. President Trump is someone that believes in the games of power, and China showed its hand. That was very critical. So the fact that the two countries are smiling at each other and shaking hands certainly does not mean that this will be a romantic honeymoon going forward. But at least before the midterm, the bilateral relationship will be very stable. Depending on how the midterm goes, President Trump may adopt a different strategy on China, or maybe not. For instance, Russia, Ukraine, Israel, Palestine, and Iran—all of these are distracting the US from its priority on China. It's got its hands so full at the moment; it's got no attention or ability, really, to take on China. That is just trade, for instance. The bilateral relationship goes so much further beyond just trade: there's technology rivalry, military security rivalry, and financial rivalry. So I believe the ongoing frictions between the US and China will be one of the key defining themes in the world. So it's not ended. That story is not ended. Oh no, this is just the beginning. Do you think in about five years from now, when the Americans fix their vulnerability on rare earths, that fear of China will certainly go away? To start with, there is no way that the US will be able to catch up with China's level of dominance on rare earths. Is that so? In five years? When we talk about the dominance in rare earths, there is the access to the resources themselves and the capacity to produce and process them. China has the absolute dominant power on both sides. So the US will not be able to replace China for at least another period of time. That's why people are concerned about the interdependence of supply chains. Can I just say it's actually very important to know that China didn't want to play the rare earth card? Europe is quite concerned about China continuing to use this. Let me emphasize again: China did not want to play the rare earth card. If it wanted to, it would have played it a long time ago. It's almost public knowledge. Chairman Deng Xiaoping said decades ago that the Middle East has oil and China has rare earths. China could have played this 40 years ago; it could have played this in the first Trump administration; it did not. China played the rare earth card when the back-and-forth of the tariff war negotiations between the two countries was just dragging on and on. That was when China decided enough is enough. China never wanted to, because look at the consequences: Europe and many other regions got scared. If anything, China is still very committed to economic globalization because China benefits from that, so China doesn't want any trade wars whatsoever. Do you think China has a similar dominance in Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs) as they have in rare earths? I'm not an expert on those specific industries, but my limited knowledge is that it is not, but it is certainly getting more power than before. One of my favorite examples is a Chinese company whose founder was Harvard or MIT-educated; she set up the company in Boston, a brilliant hub for biomedicine. Especially during the COVID time, she came back, and over time she took the team back with her. So now their headquarters are in China with all the R&D centers in China, and only the sales and marketing teams are in the US because they know the language. This is a great example. People think about Chinese manufacturing: a few years ago it was about EVs, then it was AI, and now we're looking at robotics. The next is very likely to be biotech. I did want to ask you about the gender gap. The World Economic Forum produces every year the Global Gender Gap Report. I know it's something you've taken a great interest in. You mentioned your book on the subject has become so successful—what's happened to it? Yes, I wrote a book on gender economics in Mandarin at the moment. It got so popular that there are pirated copies now. People are clamoring for it because the rise of female power is one of the key defining themes of the century, and the rise of China is another one. How do you tell companies or governments that it's in their interest to address the gender gap? Well, because it's good for the women, the men, the children, and the economy. Let me take one example. Studies looking at the world in the past 46 years show that if you put a dollar of investment into US bonds and the stock market, your annual return was 2.4%. But if you invest that in people's education, every additional year of education gives an average return of about 8.8%. That means investing into human capital gives you a much higher yield than financial capital. Within this 8.8%, the returns to women's education are 10%. On top of that, there's a study in the US showing that when a wife's education grows by an additional year, the husband makes an additional 18% to 23%. So that's great for the men as well. When women are better educated, the kids are taller, they're healthier, and they have better performance at school. So that's good for the next generation, too. It is actually very good for economic growth. According to UN Women, there is a finding suggesting that when we invest more in women, that can significantly increase the global GDP by about 4 trillion US dollars by 2030. That is more than 3% of the current GDP. So that's why I say educating women is great for everybody: for women, for the men, for the kids, and for society and the economy. But Chen, you were born in Shandong, right? Yes. And that is known to be a fairly traditional Chinese society. Did you get lucky? Where did you get your fantastic education in Europe? How did you manage to do it? Were your parents far-sighted or did they just want you to have the best? Yes. I was born in Qingdao, Shandong, where Confucius came from. It's very traditional. Even nowadays in certain parts of Shandong, women are not allowed to dine at the same table with men, and whenever we go out, you're supposed to give men "face," let them talk more, and let them take the lead. You smile and collapse when they do the talk. I had the opportunity to study in Sweden, one of the most gender-equal countries in the world, and also at UC Berkeley, the most progressive university. I got very lucky that I saw that gender equality can be very different and that it is possible. One of my PhD theses was about labor economics. I wanted to study if it is true that when women are better educated, we have a lower marriage rate. Back then, there was a saying in China that there are three kinds of people in the world: a man, a woman, and a woman who has a PhD. I was getting my PhD, and there was another saying that there are four kinds: a man, a woman, a woman who has a PhD, and a man who dares to marry the third type. I wanted to prove them wrong. Over time, I've spent more than a decade writing on the topic of gender economics, and I'm very lucky because my parents believed in the power of education. Do you think there might be a future book from you called "A Man's Place is in the Home"? I like the idea, but I want to rephrase it to "A Man's Place is Wherever He Wishes." When we talk about gender equality, the idea is not to let women become the more senior species. We want equality. The definition of gender equality is not about the same results, but that we have the same access and same opportunity. Men can cry, men can wear pink, however you want—you can play with Barbie dolls—and we can also become superwomen. I'm wearing pink. Did you see that? Did you see her? I'm still blue. I think I'll change to pink one of these days. Lovely. Thank you very much. And I did not mean that as objectifying you. At least I've not cried during this interview. We have to bring it to a close there. That's all we've got time for. Ravi, it's been great to co-host this with you. If your listeners want to follow Radio Davos, they can find us on any podcast app. We have three weekly podcasts: Radio Davos, Meet the Leader, and Agenda Dialogues. Ravi, where can we find you? Robin, that's been a fantastic conversation, and thank you, Chen, for a brilliant presentation. We didn't talk enough about concubines yet, but maybe we'll keep that for the next session. This is Ravi Velour for the Straits Times Asian Insider podcast. That was a great pleasure. Thank you for having me today. Thank you.

When to Build Your Own Agent Harness | Harrison Chase, LangChain

Sequoia Capital(236.0K)·23:57·2.2K views·2026-08-13
Harrison ChaseCEO, LangChain
TL;DRHarrison Chase explains that building an agent requires balancing three core components—harness, model, and context—and advocates starting with general off-the-shelf harnesses before customizing via middleware as tasks move out-of-distribution.
The Three Core Pillars: According...

TL;DR

Harrison Chase explains that building an agent requires balancing three core components—harness, model, and context—and advocates starting with general off-the-shelf harnesses before customizing via middleware as tasks move out-of-distribution.

Anatomy of an Agent: Harness, Model, and Context

  • The Three Core Pillars: According to LangChain, every agent consists of a harness, a model, and context; owning your intelligence requires owning all three parts.
      • Model Flexibility: Being able to switch between open weight and proprietary models avoids vendor lock-in and ensures you always use the best available model.
      • Context Management: Managing memory, semantic knowledge, and previous conversations helps personalize and guide the agent's behavior.
      • Harness Orchestration: The harness acts as the orchestrator, bringing dynamic and fixed context into the model's context window at the right point in time.
  • The Core Agent Loop: At its simplest, an agent is an LLM running in a loop that calls tools, evaluates observations, and passes them back to the model.

Customizing Harnesses with Middleware and Model Profiles

  • Using Middleware for Customization: Developers can modify the base agent loop using small middleware constructs, hooks, and plugins without rewriting the core architecture.
      • Enabled Capabilities: Middleware allows harnesses to connect to sandboxes, file systems, sub-agents, memory, and summarization steps.
      • Context Offloading: Large tool calls can be wrapped and dumped to manage context length efficiently.
  • In-Distribution vs. Out-of-Distribution Tasks: The more out-of-distribution a task is, the more customization your harness requires.
      • Keeping In-Distribution Sub-Tasks Close to the Model: Even on custom out-of-distribution tasks (like legal AI), foundational sub-tasks like file editing should mirror what the model was explicitly RL'd on.
      • Model Profiles: Deep Agents utilizes model profiles to dynamically switch between different file-edit implementations depending on whether an OpenAI or Anthropic model is active.

Evals, Benchmarks, and Observability

  • Setting Private Evals: Mirroring Satya's framing, organizations must create private evaluations to define what "good" looks like and build a continuous hill-climbing improvement loop.
  • Harbor as an Open-Source Standard: Harbor has emerged as an industry-standard open-source eval runner (created by the makers of Terminal Bench 2) used to run data sets in sandboxed Dockerfile environments.
  • Comprehensive Benchmarking: Benchmarks should track not just accuracy, but also latency and token cost across different agent harnesses, models, and reasoning efforts.
  • Observability and Trajectories: Observability is critical for debugging why an LLM call failed—usually resulting from poor context accumulation rather than an inadequate model.
      • Message Trajectories: Agents run primarily on message trajectories (human inputs followed by underlying tool-call messages and responses).
      • Debugging Views: Platforms like LangSmith provide both user-friendly summarized views and deep trace views to inspect exact model inputs and outputs.

The Data Flywheel and Automated Harness Engineering

  • Continuous Improvement Recipe: Build an agent, collect traces, curate trace data, and run experiments to update any part of the agent (harness, model, or context).
  • Feedback Mechanisms: Collecting high-quality feedback relies on clever UX design to capture implicit user signals, combined with fast, low-cost synthetic feedback from fine-tuned Small Language Models (SLMs) acting as judges.
  • LangSmith Engine: LangChain built Engine—an internal background agent that sits on top of LangSmith traces, runs using a coding agent with a LangSmith CLI, and automatically generates issue boards, prompt updates, and harness code changes.

Notable Quotes

"The main job of a harness is to bring context to the model at the right point in time." — Harrison Chase
"Create your private evals because eval defines what good looks like inside the organization." — Satya (cited by Harrison Chase)
Harnesses. I think this is a very important topic. A lot of you are thinking through building your own harnesses right now. I'm very excited to introduce Harrison. I first noticed Harrison on Twitter in 2022, back in the GPT-3 era. And Harrison was one of the first people thinking about, "Okay, we have these models. How can we build an entire harness around them so that they're not just auto-complete tasks, but that they start acting as virtual collaborators or agents?" Harrison, the ecosystem has grown so much since 2022, and I've seen you also grow a lot in terms of how you think about building agents, building harnesses, how to eval them, etc. So, I'm very excited to have you talk today. I think the talk's going to be both about harnesses and evals. The format again will be 15 minutes or so of presentation content, 15 minutes of Q&A. Thanks for joining us, Harrison. Cool. My name's Harrison, co-founder and CEO of LangChain. I want to talk about evals and harnesses in the context of kind of owning your own intelligence. So, when we talk about intelligence, we're normally talking about agents. What exactly makes up an agent? At LangChain, we think there's kind of like three main parts. There's a harness that orchestrates a model and some context. And if you're talking about owning your intelligence in general, you probably want to own all three parts of these. Owning the model, I'm not going to talk too much about. Lin was here from Fireworks talking about open weight models and owning that. A big part of this is also the ability to switch models. There used to be this concept of being like cloud agnostic and being able to switch clouds back in the day. The same thing exists, but for models. You want to be able to switch to avoid lock-in, but also to just use the best model when it's available. Context: you want to own all the context that your agent uses, whether that is memory, whether that is semantic knowledge, or whether that is previous conversations. These can help personalize and guide the agent as it goes along. And then the last bit is the harness, and that's what I really want to focus on. So, how do you really own your harness? What does that even mean? What's the main job of a harness? The main job of a harness is to bring context to the model at the right point in time. It does all the orchestration around the fixed context and the dynamic context. It brings it into the context window of the model, shows it something, gets some response, and then does something with that. Agents need to do all these different things in order to accomplish their jobs. There's a ton of domain-specific stuff that they need to do as well, but they need to interact with external systems. These external systems, when you interact with them, emit more context that can get fed back into the agent into the loop. The harness is the thing that really orchestrates all of this together. Agents, at their simplest—when everyone talks about agents, what they really talk about is just an LLM running in a loop calling tools. This is a really simple, but really general architecture. Some request comes in, the LLM makes some generation. That generation may include a tool to call. If it does, you invoke those tools, and you pass that observation back to the LLM. This is the core architecture behind pretty much every agent out there today, but they're all different in slightly different ways. We built LangChain, which is a really base minimal harness. Then there's Deep Agents. Deep Agents is our model-agnostic and more general-purpose version. It does more things; it connects to file systems, it has skills, and it has sub-agents. It's built on top of this really simple harness, but we customize it by using levers. You can run particular code snippets before the agent's invoked, before each model call. You can wrap these model calls, you can wrap the tool calls, and you can customize this core simple loop in a lot of powerful ways just by using what we call middleware constructs. There's other ways to customize the harness as well, but this has emerged as a concept of hooks and plugins in a lot of the coding agents, which is essentially what they're doing. They're taking this base loop that's running, and they're adding little hooks or plugins at various points to let you customize it. A lot of the stuff you can do is all done by that concept of middleware, by modifying that core loop. The agent's still running in a loop, it's still doing that same simple architecture, but through that you can give it access to a sandbox, a file system, sub-agents, memory, or summarization. Summarization would come in before the model. Before the model's invoked, you check if the context is too long, and then you summarize it. You can add that into this core loop through this concept of middleware. The same applies with context offloading, which is a way of basically taking large tool calls and dumping them. That wraps the tool call. The point is there's this really simple general architecture of an agent, and all of these more advanced agent harnesses are basically doing that loop but adding in a bunch of stuff while still running the core loop. As you think about building or customizing your own harness, these are the different places that you can insert things into. You can add your own summarization step, or you can add your own handling of particular tool calls. That's one way that you can customize the agent and the harness to your particular domain. The other way that you can customize the harnesses is by having a more explicit cognitive architecture. This used to be the way that a lot of people would build agents in 2023 and 2024 because the models weren't good enough to run in a loop. In order to get it to do particular things, you would have these very bespoke cognitive architectures. For example, a deep research bot would generate some sub-questions, fan them out, and then go and execute them. Then there's the code review bot, where you can see those very bespoke steps. A lot of this has gone into the harness now. While it's still this core loop, these might be added as particular modifications to that core loop. For a lot of very particular flows, we do see people still using cognitive architectures to really guide it in specific ways. One thing that we recommend to people is to start with a general harness. That's the easiest to get started with and will be quickest to time-to-value. As you narrow in on the use case that you want to be excellent at, you can start to add more of these gates and checks around it to guide it into particular ways. One question that we get a lot is when to think about building your own harness versus using an off-the-shelf one. A lot of the off-the-shelf harnesses work with particular models, like Claude Code for Anthropic or Codex for OpenAI. I think this is a big open question in the industry. My answer generally is that the more in-distribution you are of what the models are trained on, the better the off-the-shelf harness will be. As soon as you start to move further and further out of distribution, you'll probably want to tune your harness in some way. There are different ways to tune it as well. The models may be in-distribution on particular things you are doing, even if it's an out-of-distribution task. For example, in legal AI, there are things that are still in-distribution for the main models. Editing files is something that all the main models have been RL'd on. They've been RL'd in very particular ways. OpenAI and Claude models edit files in different ways in their harnesses, and as a result, their models are best at editing files in different ways. The models themselves are out-of-distribution on the larger task of legal AI, but they are in-distribution on the task of editing files. If you are building a harness there, you'll probably want a custom harness, but you'll want it to use the edit-file tool that is in-distribution for the model you're using. One of the things we do in Deep Agents, which is our customizable harness, is we have this concept of model profiles. For things that are in-distribution, we switch between different edit-file implementations depending on which model is being used. I think that's an example of customizing the overall harness when it's out-of-distribution, but keeping smaller in-distribution parts as close to the model layer as possible. The second big part of what I want to talk about is evals and observability. As you're experimenting with all parts of an agent—the model, the harness, or the context—you're going to want to know what's going on inside the system and be able to evaluate it. These are useful tools not just for custom harnesses, but also for custom models. There was a great Twitter article that Satya wrote two weeks ago where he talked about a lot of these concepts. There are three quotes in particular that stood out for me: one, "Create your private evals because eval defines what good looks like inside the organization"; two, "Retain ownership of your organization's memory, traces, feedback, decisions, and institutional context"; and three, "You create your own continuous learning loop, a hill-climbing machine that will allow your AI investments to compound the value of your firm." I think these speak to the importance of evals and observability and the learning loop they power in owning your intelligence and compounding it. Every company building a mission-critical agent will build benchmarks for that agent. You can use it to define and catch regressions, or you can hill-climb on that benchmark by adjusting the harness or the model. Things we see becoming the industry standard for defining these benchmarks include Harbor, an open-source eval runner created by the makers of Terminal Bench 2. It’s become pretty popular for a variety of domains. It lets you compare different agent harnesses, models, and reasoning efforts to see how they perform on your task. What exactly is Harbor? It's pretty simple at a high level. It consists of an agent that you run against a dataset. A dataset has a bunch of different tasks. Generally, they are run in sandboxes because you might want to parallelize them, and each task has its own environment. This is what a Harbor task looks like: it has an environment where you define the environment the agent runs in, like a Dockerfile. There is a solution, which is a golden solution used to sanity-check, and then a test, which is the verifier. The test scripts can do anything: they can run code, unit tests, or another LLM as a judge. You basically define how the agent is scored in this test. Finally, instruction.md is the prompt given to the agent. That's the core of Harbor. You define these tasks, bundle them up, run them against agents, and score how they do. When you do that, you get results you can compare. This is LangSmith, the platform we build for evals and observability. You can see experiments, feedback scores, and track latency and tokens. Observability sounds basic, but I think it's really important and underrated for agents. When agents mess up, it's either because the model isn't good enough or the context the LLM received isn't good enough. I think the second one more often than not causes issues. Having observability into what is going into the context window and how that context is accumulated—what steps were run, what tools were run—is important for debugging. Most agent paths come in the form of trajectories, which are lists of messages. When you run an agent, you type a human message, it makes tool calls, and then it responds. That message trajectory is becoming a central part of these agents. But that's not enough to fully debug it, so we have a full trace where you can click into particular things and see exactly what goes on inside the model. Evals and observability let you set up this data flywheel to compound intelligence as you and your users get feedback. A high-level recipe for continuously improving agents is simple: build an agent, run it, collect traces, curate the trace data, and run experiments on that data. Feedback is a big part of this, whether from the environment or a synthetic source. I think UX design is actually underestimated in agent design; if you present the agent in an intelligent way, you can end up getting a lot of feedback from users without them needing to click buttons explicitly. You can also run online evaluators—what we call LLM-as-a-judge—over traces to judge things cheaply and quickly. When you use that data to update what happens, you can update any part of the agent: the harness by doing harness engineering, the model by doing fine-tuning, or the context by doing memory. The part we think most about at LangChain is the harness engineering part. I want to show a quick demo of what we call LangSmith Engine, which is an agent that sits on top of your traces and does this work of curating traces, running experiments, and suggesting fixes. This is LangSmith. We have this tab called Engine. It runs in the background and creates issue boards. It’s a coding agent with access to our LangSmith CLI that helps it explore data and identify issues. Here it has created an issue with a description, links to supporting evidence, and suggested updates to prompts, context, or even code to go into the harness. This speaks to the data flywheel: run the agent, get traces, see patterns, and fix. This is our attempt at automating it. Happy to take any questions on harnesses or evals. This is great, by the way. Engine itself is an agent, right? That's given a prompt to go search over things. Have you run Engine on Engine? We have it running, yeah. Engine also hooks up to Slack and sends reports about itself, and that's how we dogfood it. We also created what we call Issue Bench for Engine, which is a Harbor-formatted benchmark. We are constantly benchmarking different models and different harnesses on it. One of the benefits of having a benchmark is that you can see what different harnesses are good and bad at. We ran our own Deep Agents, Codex, and Claude Code on this. We saw that Codex was doing an interesting thing where it would write itself small scripts to run against traces, which allowed it to perform really well. We did a sprint to do what we call the "codexification" of Engine to bring that learning into the core harness. Having a benchmark allows you to see how different things perform and bring those lessons back into your core agent harness. To what extent do you think that harnesses will converge into one thing, versus diversifying where every company has their own way of doing things? Really good question. I don't know the honest answer. I think general-purpose harnesses have gotten good enough to work for a lot of basic tasks. I would recommend getting started with an off-the-shelf harness because the models are good enough and we've learned what makes them work—access to file systems, sub-agents, etc. However, the more out-of-distribution you get, the more you're going to want to customize the harness. There is a spectrum. At the extreme end, you might build a complete cognitive architecture for predictability and control. We have customers in financial services who find a standard agent "too scary" and want more control. At the other end, you can use an off-the-shelf harness. The model labs are converging on being very good at coding, and harnesses are converging to be good at coding as well. But there are still small differences in how they implement things like file editing. I don't know how that will play out, but that's why evals and observability are important—so you can measure all of it. Cool. Awesome. Thank you guys.
